increasing paths, each of which having probability

p^{k}
to be open in the Barak-Erdős graph. Therefore,

\mathbb{E}(Z_{k}(n,p))=\binom{n}{k+1}p^{k}\approx\frac{n^{k+1}p^{k}}{k!}.

With similar computations, we have that

\mathbb{E}(Z_{k}(n,p)^{2})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{k+1}\leq n\\
1\leq j_{1}\leq\cdots\leq j_{k+1}\leq n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}((i_{1},i_{2}),\ldots(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in E(G)).

Observing that if two paths have
r
vertices in common,
they have at most
r-1
edges in common, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Z_{k}(n,p)^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\binom{n}{k+1}\binom{n-k-1}{k+1}p^{2k}+\sum_{r=1}^{k+1}\binom{n}{k+1}\binom{n-k-1}{k-r+1}p^{2k-r-1}

\displaystyle\approx(n^{k+1}p^{k})^{2}+\sum_{r=1}^{k+1}n^{2k+2-r}p^{2k-r-1}.

If
n^{2}p_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we have

\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})\geq 1)=\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}(n,p_{n})\geq 1)\leq\frac{n(n-1)}{2}p_{n}.

Therefore
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})=0)=1
, showing that
L_{n}(p_{n})\to 0
in probability.

We now assume that there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{n}n^{1+1/m}=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{n}n^{1+1/(m+1)}=0.

We observe that

\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})\geq m+1)=\mathbb{P}(Z_{m+1}(n,p_{n})\geq 1)\leq\mathbb{E}(Z_{m+1}(n,p_{n}))\leq\binom{n}{m+1}p_{n}^{m}.

Then, as
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{m+1}p_{n}^{m}=0
, we deduce that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})\geq m+1)=0
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})\geq m)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Z_{m}(n,p_{n})\geq 1)\geq\mathbb{E}(Z_{m}(n,p_{n}))^{2}/\mathbb{E}(Z_{m}(n,p_{n})^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+\sum_{r=1}^{m+1}\frac{\binom{n-k-1}{k-r+1}}{\binom{n-k-1}{k+1}}p_{n}^{-r-2}}.

But as
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-r}p_{n}^{-r-2}=0
for all
r\leq m+1
by assumption, we obtain that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})\geq m)=1
, completing the proof that
L_{n}(p_{n})\to m
in probability in that case.

Next, assuming that
p_{n}^{m}n^{m+1}\to\theta^{m}
, we show that
Z_{m}(n,p_{n})

converges to a Poisson random variable with parameter
\theta^{m}/(m+1)!
. Indeed,

Z_{m}(n,p_{n})
is the sum of
\binom{n}{m+1}
Bernoulli random variables with
parameter
\theta^{m}/n^{m+1}
. We then observe that the sum of the covariances of
these Bernoulli random variables converges to
0
as
n\to\infty
, proving
this convergence. As a result, we obtain

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})\geq m)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(Z_{m}(n,p_{n})\geq 1)=1-e^{-\theta^{m}/m!},

and as
\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})\geq m-1)\to 1
using the same computations as above, we have obtained the convergence in law of
L_{n}(p_{n})
.
∎

We then consider the asymptotic behavior of
L_{n}(p_{n})
assuming that
np_{n}=o(\log n)
. In this regime, recall that the length of the longest path issued
from vertex
1
is of order
enp_{n}=o(\log n)
. The following result holds.

Theorem 5.21
.

Let
(p_{n})
be a null sequence such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{1+\varepsilon}p_{n}=\infty
for all
\varepsilon>0
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}np_{n}/\log n=0
. We
set

\ell_{n}=\sup\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\binom{n}{k}p_{n}^{k}\leq 1\right\},

then
\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{n}(p_{n})/\ell_{n}=1
in probability.

This result is obtained in the same way as Theorem
5.20
, using
first and second moment methods to bound the probability of existence of a path
of length
\ell_{n}(1\pm\varepsilon)
. Using similar methods again, one can turn
to the case
p_{n}\sim\gamma\log n/n
. In this situation,
L^{(1)}_{n}(p_{n})\sim\gamma e\log n
, and the longest path also is of order
\log n
, but with a
larger constant.

Theorem 5.22
.

Let
(p_{n})
be a sequence such that
p_{n}\sim\gamma\log n/n
as
n\to\infty

for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{n}(p_{n})/np_{n}=eA(\gamma)\quad\text{in probability},

where
A(\gamma)
is the only solution larger than
1
of

x\log x=(e\gamma)^{-1}
.

Finally, when
np_{n}\gg\log n
, the length of the longest path and the longest
path starting from
1
have similar orders of magnitude.

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
(p_{n})
be a null sequence such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}np_{n}/\log n=\infty
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{n}(p_{n})/np_{n}=e.

To prove that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(L_{n}(p_{n})>np_{n}e)=0
, we use again first moment estimates. Using that
L_{n}(p_{n})\geq L_{n}^{(1)}(p_{n})
on the other hand and Theorem
5.15
, we conclude
to Theorem
5.23
.

5.7
Shortest path

Based on similar computations with the previous section, we are able to compute
the asymptotic behavior of the length of the
shortest
path between
1

and
n
in the Barak-Erdős graph
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}(n,p)
.
The results in this section are based on
[
81
]
.
In
this section, we write

S_{n}(p)=\min\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\exists i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k-1}:(1,i_{1}),\ldots,(i_{k-1},n)\in E(\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}(n,p))\right\}.

the length of the shortest path linking
1
and
n
. By convention,
S_{n}=\infty
if there is no path between
1
and
n
.

Observe that for a fixed value of
p
, the edge
(1,n)
is present with
probability
p
, and if this edge is not present, the events

(\{(1,k),(k,n)\in E(\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}(n,p))\},2\leq k\leq n)

are independent of one another, and with same probability
p^{2}
of occurrence.
As a result, we have

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}(p)=1)=p,\quad\mathbb{P}(S_{n}(p)=2)=(1-p)(1-(1-p^{2})^{n-2}).

Therefore,
S_{n}(p)
converges in distribution, as
n\to\infty
, to a random
variable
S_{\infty}(p)
with
\mathbb{P}(S_{\infty}(p)=1)=1-\mathbb{P}(S_{\infty}(p)=2)=p
.
With similar computations, it appears that if
p_{n}\to 0
with
np_{n}^{2}\to\infty
, then
S_{n}(p_{n})\to 2
in probability as
n\to\infty
.

The above computation extends to larger paths, and we obtain the following
extension of the results of Section
5.6
.

Theorem 5.24
.

Let
(p_{n})
be a null sequence.

1.

If there exists an integer
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{n}n^{1-1/(m+1)}=\infty\quad\text{with}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{n}n^{1-1/m}=0,

then
S_{n}(p_{n})\to m+1
in probability.

2.

If there exists an integer
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{n}n^{1-1/m}=\theta\in\mathbb{R}_{+},

then
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(S_{n}(p_{n})=m)=1-\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(S_{n}(p_{n})=m+1)=e^{-\theta^{m}/(m-1)!}

3.

If
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{1-\varepsilon}p_{n}=\infty
for all
\varepsilon>0
, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}S_{n}(p_{n})/\ell_{n}=1
in probability, where
\ell_{n}
is the same quantity as in Theorem
5.21
.

For a graph with too small value of
p
, the vertices
1
and
n
will no longer
be connected with high probability. It is well-known for Erdős-Rényi graphs
that as long as
p_{n}>(1+\varepsilon)/n
, vertices
1
and
n
will be in the same
connected component with positive probability. However, this does not implies
the existence of a directed path from
1
to
n
in the graph, and in fact the
coupling with the branching random walk shows that as long as
\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}np_{n}<\infty
, the probability that
1
and
n
are in the same
connected component goes to
0
as
n\to\infty
.

The critical decay rate at which the probability that
1
and
n
are connected
in
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}(n,p_{n})
with positive probability is currently
unknown, as well as the asymptotic behavior of
S_{n}(p_{n})
for
p_{n}\sim(\log n)^{\alpha}/n
for some
\alpha>0
.

6
Regenerative properties of directed random graphs

We now return to the setup of Section
2.1

and consider the case where we are given a sequence
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots

of numbers in
[0,1]
such that

0<p_{1}<1.

The case
p_{1}=1
is excluded because it uninteresting.
We assume that
p_{1}>0
so that the regenerative methods work:
as will be shown in Section
6.1
, we can then break the graph into i.i.d. pieces.
For the
p_{1}=0
case see Remark
6.11
below.

We then consider the random graph on
\mathbb{Z}
such
that the pair
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
is a vertex if
i<j
and with probability

p_{j-i}
, independently from pair to pair.
Let us denote this graph by
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},(p_{j}))
.
The reason for introducing probabilities that depend on the physical
distance between vertices is twofold: from the point of view of applications,
it is more natural; mathematically, the case of constant
p_{j}
, insofar as
regenerative properties are concerned, is not much different from the more
general case.
Let

q_{j}:=1-p_{j},\quad Q_{j}:=q_{1}\cdots q_{j},\quad Q_{0}:=1.

Our goal is to prove a functional central limit theorem for the
quantity
L_{0,n}
, the maximum length of all pathr in
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},(p_{j}))

with endpoints between
0
and
n
.
This is Theorem
6.9
.
To do this, we first exhibit some regenerative properties of the graph
and then estimate moments of the distances between two typical skeleton points.

6.1
Breaking the graph into independent cycles

We showed in Lemma
2.6
,
that if the sequence
p_{j}
does not convergence to
0
too fast,
in the sense that condition (
2.7
) of Lemma
2.6
,

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}Q_{n}<\infty,

holds, then the skeleton set
\mathscr{S}

of all vertices
v
such that
u\leadsto v\leadsto w

for all
u<v<w
(where
i\leadsto j
stands for “there is
a path from
i
to
j
”)
is infinite in both directions a.s. and has strictly positive rate

\lambda=\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}(1-Q_{j})^{2}>0,

(6.1)

as in (
2.8
).
We label the elements of
\mathscr{S}
by random integers
\Gamma_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
so that

\cdots<\Gamma_{-1}<\Gamma_{0}\leq 0<\Gamma_{1}<\Gamma_{2}<\cdots

(6.2)

We therefore know that
\mathbb{P}(0\in\mathscr{S})=\lambda>0
and

\mathbb{E}(\Gamma_{k+1}-\Gamma_{k}|0\in\mathscr{S})=\mathbb{E}(\Gamma_{k+1}-\Gamma_{k}|\Gamma_{0}=0)=1/\lambda<\infty,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Consider now the random sequence

\alpha^{(0)}:=(\alpha^{(0)}_{1},\alpha^{(0)}_{2},\ldots),

consisting of independent entries with

\mathbb{P}(\alpha^{(0)}_{j}=1)=1-\mathbb{P}(\alpha^{(0)}_{j}=-\infty)=p_{j},\quad j\in\mathbb{N},

and let
\alpha^{(n)}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, be i.i.d. copies of
\alpha^{(0)}
.
Then we can construct
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},(p_{j}))
as the graph on
\mathbb{Z}

with edge set

\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}:\,i<j,\,\alpha^{(i)}_{j-i}=1\}.

(Note that the random variable
\alpha^{(i)}_{j-i}
was denoted by

\alpha_{i,j}
in (
2.2
).)
We refer to the random object

\mathscr{C}_{k}:=(\alpha^{(n)},\,\Gamma_{k}\leq n<\Gamma_{k+1})

as the
k
th “cycle”,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\overrightarrow{G}_{k}
be the induced subgraph of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},(p_{j}))

on the set of vertices
\{v\in\mathbb{Z}:\,\Gamma_{k}\leq v\leq\Gamma_{k+1}\}
,
that is, keep only those edges with endpoints in this set.
The following was proved in
[
39
]
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Assume that (
2.7
) holds. Then,
conditional on
\{0\in\mathscr{S}\}
, the cycles
\mathscr{C}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, are i.i.d. and the random graphs
\overrightarrow{G}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, are i.i.d.

Sketch of proof.

It suffices to show that
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is independent of

(\mathscr{C}_{-1},\mathscr{C}_{-2},\ldots)
conditional on
0\in\mathscr{S}
.
Let
\mathscr{F}^{+}:=\sigma(\alpha^{(n)},n>0)
,

\mathscr{F}^{-}:=\sigma(\alpha^{(n)},n<0)
.
As in the proof of Lemma
2.6
,
for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, consider the largest of the vertices
u<j
such that

(u,j)
is an edge, letting
\ell(j)=j-u
be its distance from
j
.
Similarly, consider the smallest of the vertices
v>j
such that
(j,v)

is an edge, setting
r(j)=v-j
.
Note that

\{0\in\mathscr{S}\}=\{\Gamma_{0}=0\}=\{r(-1)\leq 1,r(-2)\leq 2,\ldots;\ell(1)\leq 1,\ell(2)\leq 2,\ldots\}

(6.3)

Define the random variables

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}_{-1}

\displaystyle:=\max\{n<0:r(n-1)\leq 1,r(n-2)\leq 2,\ldots;\ell(n+1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(0)\leq|n|\}

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\min\{n>0:~r(0)\leq n,\ldots,r(n-1)\leq 1;\ell(n+1)\leq 1,\ell(n+2)\leq 2,\ldots\}

and observe that

\text{ if }\Gamma_{0}=0\text{ then }\Gamma_{-1}=\widehat{\Gamma}_{-1},\,\Gamma_{1}=\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}.

Whereas
\Gamma_{-1}
is not
\mathscr{F}^{-}
–measurable,
the random variable
\widehat{\Gamma}_{-1}
is.
Also,
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}
is
\mathscr{F}^{+}
measurable.
Similarly, each cycle
\mathscr{C}_{k}
with negative index
k

becomes equal to an
\mathscr{F}^{-}
–measurable random object.
This argument shows that
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is independent of

(\mathscr{C}_{-1},\mathscr{C}_{-2},\ldots)
, conditional on
\{\Gamma_{0}=0\}
.
That all the
\mathscr{C}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
are identically distributed, under the same conditioning, follows from stationarity.
For the last claim
observe that
\overrightarrow{G}_{k}
is a function of
\mathscr{C}_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Let
\alpha
denote the map
n\mapsto\alpha^{(n)}
.
For all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, denote by
\theta^{k}\alpha

the map
n\mapsto\alpha^{(n+k)}
.
Note that the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
relates to
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|0\in\mathscr{S})

as follows. Let
\Phi(\alpha)
be a measurable bounded function of
\alpha
.
Denote by
\theta^{k}\Phi
the function
\alpha\mapsto\Phi(\theta^{k}\alpha)
.
Then

\mathbb{E}(\Phi)=\lambda\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\sum_{\Gamma_{0}\leq k<\Gamma_{1}}\theta^{k}\Phi\bigg|0\in\mathscr{S}\bigg\}.

For a simple proof of this, see
[
69
]
.
This implies that (without conditioning on
0\in\mathscr{S}
), the cycles

\mathscr{C}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, are i.i.d. each
with the same distribution as the conditional distribution of
\mathscr{C}_{0}

given
0\in\mathscr{S}
. Moreover,
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is independent of the rest of the
cycles, and its law can be found by the last formula.
In particular,
\mathbb{E}(\Gamma_{k+1}-\Gamma_{k})=\mathbb{E}(\Gamma_{k+1}-\Gamma_{k}|\Gamma_{0}=0)=1/\lambda
for all
k\neq 0
.
For
k=0
, letting
\Phi=\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0}
in the above formula,
we have
\mathbb{E}(\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0})=\lambda\mathbb{E}(\Gamma_{1}^{2}|\Gamma_{0}=0)

may be equal to
\infty

unless a condition stronger that (
2.7
) is assumed.

6.2
Moments of auxiliary stopping times

We aim at studying the moments of the auxiliary random vertices

\mu
and
\nu
, defined in (
6.5
) and (
6.8
),
respectively. These random vertices are positive integers
and stopping times with respect to the filtration, in the index
n
, generated
by the (
\alpha^{(k)}
,
k\leq n
).
The stopping time property is crucial in constructing,
in Section
6.3
, certain iterates of
\mu
and
\nu
,
used for identifying the least positive skeleton point of the graph.

We need some notation, partially introduced in the proof of Lemma
2.6
.
Recalling that
i\leadsto j
means that there is a path
in
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},\alpha)
from
i
to
j
, we let
J\subset\mathbb{Z}

and write
i\leadsto J
if
i\leadsto j
for all
j\in J
.
Similarly,
J\leadsto i
means
j\leadsto i
for all
j\in J
.
We will need the events

\displaystyle\begin{split}A^{+}_{u,v}&=\{u\leadsto[u+1,v]\},\\
A^{-}_{u,v}&=\{[u,v-1]\leadsto v\},\end{split}

(6.4)

where
u<v
are integers.
By
[a,b]
when
a,b
are integers,
a<b
, we mean
the set
\{a,a+1,\ldots,b\}
.
We have

\ell(j)=\max\{k>0:\,\alpha_{j-k,j}=1\}

and
r(j)=\min\{k>0:\,\alpha_{j,j+k}=1\}
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence
j-\ell(j)
is the first predecessor of
j
in
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},(p_{j}))

and
j+r(j)
its first successor.
The
\ell(j)
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
are i.i.d. with common distribution
determined by

\mathbb{P}(\ell(0)>k)=Q_{k}=\mathbb{P}(r(0)>k).

In particular,
\mathbb{E}\ell(0)=\mathbb{E}r(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{k}
.
A few moments of reflection show that, for
d\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle A^{+}_{u,u+d}

\displaystyle=\{\ell(u+1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(u+d)\leq d\}

\displaystyle A^{-}_{u-d,u}

\displaystyle=\{r(u-1)\leq 1,\ldots,r(u-d)\leq d\}.

Both events decrease as
d
increases.
Define the random variable

\mu:=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\,A_{0,k}^{+}\text{ fails}\}

(6.5)

(where “
A
fails” stands for “
A^{c}
occurs”, that is,
{\text{\Large$\mathfrak{1}$}}_{A^{c}}=1
)
Since
A^{+}_{0,k}
decreases as
k
increases, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mu>k)=\mathbb{P}(A_{0,k}^{+})=\mathbb{P}(\ell(1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(k)\leq k)=(1-Q_{1})\cdots(1-Q_{k}),

and this implies that
\mu
is defective:

\mathbb{P}(\mu=\infty)=\lim_{k\to\infty}(1-Q_{1})\cdots(1-Q_{k})=\sqrt{\lambda}>0.

We also have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(n<\mu<\infty)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(A_{0,n}^{+}\cap\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}(A_{0,k}^{+})^{c}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(A_{0,n}^{+}\cap\bigcup_{k=n+1}^{\infty}(A_{0,k}^{+})^{c}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(A_{0,n}^{+}\cap\bigg(\bigcap_{k=n+1}^{\infty}A_{0,k}^{+}\bigg)^{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\ell(1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(n)\leq n;\{\ell(1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(n)\leq n,\ell(n+1)\leq n+1,\ldots\}^{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\ell(1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(n)\leq n;\{\ell(n+1)\leq n+1,\ell(n+2)\leq n+2,\ldots\}^{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\ell(1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(n)\leq n)\,\mathbb{P}(\exists k>n~\ell(k)>k)

(6.6)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\ell(1)\leq 1,\ldots,\ell(n)\leq n)\,\left[1-\mathbb{P}(\ell(n+1)\leq n+1,\ell(n+2)\leq n+2,\ldots)\right]

\displaystyle=(1-Q_{1})\cdots(1-Q_{n})\,\left[1-(1-Q_{n+1})(1-Q_{n+2})\cdots\right]

(6.7)

\displaystyle=(1-Q_{1})\cdots(1-Q_{n})-\sqrt{\lambda}.

We obtain an upper bound of this easily from (
6.6
):

\mathbb{P}(n<\mu<\infty)\leq\mathbb{P}(\exists k>n~\ell(k)>k)\leq\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\ell(k)>k)=\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\ell(0)>k)=\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}Q_{k},

and this upper bound converges to
0
as
n\to\infty
by
the assumption that the sequence
(Q_{n})
is summable.
We also have an asymptotic lower bound by using (
6.7
),
the inequality
(1-Q_{1})\cdots(1-Q_{n})\geq\sqrt{\lambda}
, for all
n
, and the
fact that
(1-e^{-x})/x
decreases as
x
increases:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(n<\mu<\infty)\geq\sqrt{\lambda}\left[1-e^{-(Q_{n+1}+Q_{n+2}+\cdots)}\right]\geq\sqrt{\lambda}\,\frac{1-e^{-\mathbb{E}\ell(0)}}{\mathbb{E}\ell(0)}\,\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}Q_{k}.

We have thus proved

Lemma 6.3
.

\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{1-\sqrt{\lambda}}\,\frac{1-e^{-\mathbb{E}\ell(0)}}{\mathbb{E}\ell(0)}\,\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}Q_{k}\leq\mathbb{P}(\mu>n|\mu<\infty)\leq\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\lambda}}\,\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}Q_{k}.

Since
Q_{k}=\mathbb{P}(\ell(0)>k)
, the above inequality says that
the tail of the distribution of
\mu
conditional on it being finite
is comparable to the “integrated” tail of the distribution of

\ell(0)
.
We say that a positive random variable
Z
has
p
-moment, for
p\geq 1
,
if
\mathbb{E}Z^{p}<\infty
; we say that it has an exponential moment
of
\mathbb{E}e^{\theta Z}<\infty
for some
\theta>0
.
From the above lemma we conclude the following.

Corollary 6.4
.

Conditional on
\{\mu<\infty\}
,
\mu
has a
p
-moment
(respectively, exponential moment) if and only if
\ell(0)

has a
(p+1)
-moment (respectively, exponential moment).

We similarly wish to examine the moments of the random variable

\nu:=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\,A_{0,k}^{-}\text{ occurs}\}.

(6.8)

Since
A_{0,k}^{-}
is not monotonic in
k
, we need to use an
argument different than before.
First observe that

\nu=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}\,[0,k-1]\leadsto k\}=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\,r(k-1)\leq 1,\ldots,r(0)\leq k\}.

Define a sequence of nonnegative integer-valued random variables

x_{n}
,
n=0,1,\ldots
, by
x_{0}=0
and

x_{n}=\max\{r(0)-n,r(1)-(n-1),\dots,r(n-1)-1\}.

Then

\nu=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\,x_{n}=0\}.

But the
x_{n}
satisfy

x_{n+1}=\max(x_{n},r(n))-1,\quad n\geq 0,

and since the
r(n)
are i.i.d., the sequence
(x_{n})
is Markovian.
For any integer
K>0
,
if
x_{n}\geq K
, then

\displaystyle x_{n+1}-x_{n}=(r(n)-x_{n})^{+}-1\leq(r(n)-K)^{+}-1.

Since
r(0)
has finite expectation,
we have that
\mathbb{E}(r(0)-K)^{+}<1
, for
K
large enough.
Therefore, after the Markov chain
leaves the interval
[0,K]
, it is upper-bounded by a random walk
with increments distributed like
(r(0)-K)^{+}-1
whose mean is negative.
By standard properties of random walks this implies that the
finiteness of the
p
-th moment of the return time
T_{K}

to the set
[0,K]
is eqiuvalent to the finiteness of the
p
-th moment
of the positive part of the increments and, in turn, to
the finiteness of the
p
-th moment of
r(0)
which is
the same as the
p
-th moment of
\ell(0)
. Similar conclusions
are made for exponential moments.
We have thus proved:

Lemma 6.5
.

\nu
has a
p
-moment (respectively, exponential moment) if and only if

\ell(0)
has a
p
-moment (respectively, expomential moment).

Remark 6.6
.

Since
\mathbb{P}(\ell(0)>k)=Q_{k}
,
k\geq 0
, we see that
\ell(0)
has

p
-th moment if and only iff
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{p-1}Q_{k}<\infty
.
and
\ell(0)
has an exponential moment if and only if

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}z^{k}Q_{k}<\infty
for some
z>1
.
In particular, these conditions hold if
p_{j}=p\in(0,1)
for all
j
,

6.3
Moments of skeleton points and a central limit theorem

We show that not only skeleton points exist but that they can also
be constructed recursively and causally.
This will be done by means of iterates of the
the stopping times
\mu
and
\nu
.
We define two interlaced sequences of stopping times

\nu[1]<\mu[1]<\nu[2]<\mu[2]<\nu[3]<\mu[3]<\cdots,

as follows.

\displaystyle\nu[1]

\displaystyle:=\nu

\displaystyle\mu[1]

\displaystyle:=\nu+\theta^{\nu}\mu=\inf\{j>\nu:~A^{+}_{\nu,j}\text{ fails}\},

(6.9)

and, recursively, for
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\nu[k]

\displaystyle:=\inf\{j>\mu[k-1]:A_{\nu[k-1],j}^{-}\text{ occurs}\}

\displaystyle\mu[k]

\displaystyle:=\nu[k]+\theta^{\nu[k]}\mu=\inf\{j>\nu[k]:A_{\nu[k],j}^{+}\text{ fails}\}.

(6.10)

It is understood that if for some
k

we have
\mu[k]=\infty
then
\nu[j]=\mu[j]=\infty
for all
j\geq k+1
.
In fact, since
\mu
is a defective random variable, with
\mathbb{P}(\mu=\infty)=\sqrt{\lambda}
, it follows that the recursion terminates in finitely
many steps.
Let

K:=\inf\{k\geq 1:~\mu[k]=\infty\}.

(6.11)

It is easy to see that
K
is a geometric
(\sqrt{\lambda})

random variable, that is,

\mathbb{P}(K>k)=(1-\sqrt{\lambda})^{k}
,
k\geq 0
.
By construction, we have
\nu[K]<\infty
a.s.

Remark 6.7
.

It may be instructive to provide intuition regarding the construction above.
We wish to find a vertex
K
that has the property
K\leadsto j
for all
j>K

and

i\leadsto K
for all
0\leq i<K
.
(This is not quite saying that
K
is a skeleton point because we don’t
care whether negative vertices lead to
K
via a path.)
Keep in mind that
\nu
is a.s. finite but
\mu
has positive probability
that it be infinity.
We first have that ,
A^{-}_{0,\nu}
holds. that is,
i\leadsto\nu
for all

0\leq i<\nu
.
Now define
\mu[1]
such that
\mu[1]-\nu[1]
has law of
\mu
.
Suppose it so happens that
\mu[1]=\infty
.
This means, by definition, that
A^{+}_{\nu,j}
holds for all
j>\nu
,
that is,
\nu\leadsto j
for all
j>\nu
.
And so, the vertex
K=\nu
has the required properties.
If
\mu[1]
is not
\infty
, then we repeat the procedure
as many times as is required until we obtain one that is infinity.

Using the observation that
\nu[k]
and
\mu[k]
are stopping times
with respect to the filtration
(\mathscr{F}^{-}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
where
\mathscr{F}^{-}_{n}=\sigma(\alpha^{(k)},k\leq n)
,
we easily obtain that

\nu[K]\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\nu_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa-1}\mu_{i},

where
\kappa,\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots

are independent, with
\kappa\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}K
, hence geometric
(\sqrt{\lambda})
,

\nu_{i}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\nu
, and
\mathbb{P}(\mu_{i}\in\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(\mu\in\cdot|\mu<\infty)
.

Lemma 6.8
.

\nu[K]
has a
p
-moment (respectively, exponential moment) if and only if

\ell(0)
has a
(p+1)
-moment (respectively, exponential moment).

Proof.

From Lemma
6.5
, we have that
\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\nu_{i}

has an exponential (respectively,
p
-th) moment if and only if

\ell(0)
has an exponential (respectively,
p
-th) moment.
From Corollary
6.5
, we have that
\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa-1}\mu_{i}

has an exponential (respectively,
p
-th) moment if and only if

\ell(0)
has an exponential (respectively,
(p+1)
-th) moment.
∎

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
p\geq 1
.

(i)
\mathbb{E}((\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0})^{p}|\Gamma_{0}=0)<\infty

if and only if
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{p}Q_{k}<\infty

(ii) The distribution of
\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0}
conditional on
\Gamma_{0}=0

has an exponential moment if and only if
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}z^{k}Q_{k}<\infty

for some
z>1
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\nu
and
\mu
we easily see that

\big[0,\nu[K]-1\big]\leadsto\nu[K]\leadsto\big[\nu[K]+1,\infty\big)\text{ a.s.}

In fact,
\nu[K]
is the least
j\geq 1
such that

[0,j-1]\leadsto j\leadsto[j+1,\infty)
.
Note that

(-\infty,-1]\leadsto 0\text{ and }\big[0,\nu[K]-1\big]\leadsto\nu[K]\text{ implies }(-\infty,\nu[K]-1]\leadsto\nu[K].

Hence, conditional on
0
being a skeleton point (
\Gamma_{0}=0
),
we have that
\nu[K]
is the next skeleton point:

\mathbb{P}(\nu[K]=\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0}|\Gamma_{0}=0)=1.

We now use Lemma
6.8
and Remark
6.6
to conclude.
∎

The results above are used in the proof of the following
functional central limit theorem.

Theorem 6.10
.

Consider the random graph
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},(p_{j}))
, assuming
0<p_{1}<1
,
and

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}(1-p_{1})\cdots(1-p_{k})<\infty.

For integers
i<j
, let
L_{i,j}
be the maximum length of all paths with endpoints
between
i
and
j
. Let
\Gamma_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, be the skeleton points,
assuming
\Gamma_{0}\leq 0<\Gamma_{1}
, and
C
the constant appearing
in the law of large numbers for
L_{0,n}
as in Lemma
2.1
. Then

0<\sigma^{2}:=\operatorname{var}(L_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}-C(\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}))<\infty

(6.12)

Define

\ell_{n}(t):=\frac{L_{0,[nt]}-Cnt}{\sigma\sqrt{\lambda n}},\quad t\geq 0,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Considering the sequence
\ell_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, as a sequence of
random elements of the Skorokhod space
D[0,\infty)
(see
[
16
]
),
equipped with the topology of local uniform convergence,
converges in distribution to a standard Brownian motion.

Sketch of proof.

We have
\lambda>0
because
p_{1}>0
.
It is clear that
\sigma^{2}>0
. The reason that
\sigma^{2}
is finite
follows
L_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\leq\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}
and Theorem
6.9

which guarantees that
\mathbb{E}[(\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{2})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[(\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0})^{2}|\Gamma_{0}=0]<\infty
.
From Remark
6.2
we also have that
\mathbb{E}\Gamma_{1}<\infty
.
Let
N_{n}=\max\{j\geq 1:\,\Gamma_{j}\leq n\}
.
By the definition of
\mathscr{S}
, if
v\in\mathscr{S}
then the maximal path from
some
u<v
to some
w>v
must necessarily include
v
.
Hence

L_{0,n}=L_{0,\Gamma_{1}}+L_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}+\cdots+L_{\Gamma_{N_{n},n}}.

We can then write

\ell_{n}(t)=\frac{L_{0,\Gamma_{1}}-C\Gamma_{1}}{\sigma\sqrt{\lambda n}}+\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{\lambda n}}\sum_{i=2}^{N_{nt}}\left[L_{\Gamma_{i-1},\Gamma_{i}}-C(\Gamma_{i}-\Gamma_{i-1})\right]+\frac{L_{\Gamma_{nt},nt}-C(nt-\Gamma_{N_{nt}})}{\sigma\sqrt{\lambda n}}

(6.13)

Define also

\widehat{\ell}_{n}(t):=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{\lambda n}}\sum_{i=2}^{nt}\left[L_{\Gamma_{i-1},\Gamma_{i}}-C(\Gamma_{i}-\Gamma_{i-1})\right],\quad\varphi_{n}(t):=\frac{N_{nt}}{n}.

The first and third terms of (
6.13
) converge to
0
in distribution,
as random elements of
D[0,\infty)
.
The middle term is simply equal to

\widehat{\ell}_{n}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\varphi_{n}
.
Hence the limit of
\ell_{n}
exists if and only if the limit of

\widehat{\ell}_{n}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\varphi_{n}
exists, in which case the limits are equal.
But
\widehat{\ell}_{n}
converges in distribution to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}B
,
where
B
is a standard Brownian motion, and
\varphi_{n}
converges in distribution
to the function
\varphi
, where
\varphi(t)=\lambda t
.
Therefore, by the continuity in
D[0,\infty)
, of the composition
operator, we have
\widehat{\ell}_{n}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\varphi_{n}
converges in distribution
to the process
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}B(\lambda t)
,
t\geq 0
,
which is equal in distribution to
B
.
Hence
\ell_{n}
converges in distribution to
B
.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

We made the assumption that
p_{1}>0
in order
that the set
\mathscr{S}
serve as a skeleton set over which
the graph regenerates, that is, we have independent pieces.
If
p_{1}=0
then
\mathscr{S}
is useless; for example,
\lambda=0
in this case.
However, if
p_{1}=\cdots=p_{j}=0
but
p_{j+1}>0

then we can establish a 1-dependent structure.
11
11

11

We say that
a random sequence
X_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, is 1-dependent if, for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
the sequences
(X_{j},j<i)
and
(X_{j},j>i)
are independent.

One can still deal with this case, but one needs a somewhat different technique
based on weakly regenerative processes.

Remark 6.12
.

At this stage, we have no estimate for the variance (
6.12
).
Estimating this is probably a complex problem even for the
case when all the
p_{j}
are equal.

Remark 6.13
.

Observe that in
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
, with positive probability, there are many maximal length
paths between successive skeleton points.
Since
\lambda>0
, this
implies that the number of linear extensions of the random partial order
induced by the edges on
n
vertices
increases exponentially fast, and this allows one to obtain directly the CLT
for the logarithm number of linear extensions
[
6
]
.

Remark 6.14
.

Note that the events appearing in the definition of
\mathscr{S}

depend on the entire random sequence of edges but, nevertheless,
produce a regenerative structure.
This phenomenon, in a more general context, has been studied in
[
50
]
.

7
Directed random graphs on partially ordered sets

A direction towards generalizations of the Barak-Erdős graph
on the set of integers, is to consider a set of vertices
V

equipped with a partial order. The directed random graph
must respect the partial order of
V
.
We will consider two cases below, that of
V=\mathbb{Z}\times I
where

I
is a finite set and that of
V=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
.
We shall deal with law of large numbers and functional central
limit theorems and see that asymptotic normality fails.
There is an interesting connection with Brownian last passage
percolation
[
56
,
11
]
. The Brownian last passage
percolation process is the one defined by (
7.1
) below.
The results of this section are taken from
[
39
]
and
[
72
]
.

7.1
Brownian last passage percolation

Consider
V=\mathbb{Z}\times I
where, for simplicity,
let
I=\{0,1,\ldots,M\}
for a positive integer
M
.
Elements of
V
are denoted by
(u,i)
,
(v,j)
, etc.
Define the standard partial order on
V
, denoted by
\ll
by

(u,i)\ll(v,j)\text{ if }(u,i)\neq(v,j)\text{ and }u\leq v,i\leq j.

Consider the random graph

\overrightarrow{G}(V,p)
, with
0<p<1
with edges defined as follows.
A pair of vertices
(u,i)
,
(v,j)
, with
(u,i)\ll(v,j)
,
form an edge directed from
(u,i)
to
(v,j)

with probability
p
, independently from pair to pair.
We consider all directed paths from
(u,i)
to
(v,j)
and denote
by
L_{(u,i),(v,j)}
the maximum length of all such paths.
We also let

\text{
$L^{*}_{u,v}=$ maximum length of all paths with endpoints in the
$[u,v]\times I$},\quad L^{*}_{n}:=L_{n}^{*}.

We are interested in the LLN and CLT for
L^{*}_{0,n}
as
n\to\infty
.

Regarding the LLN the ergodic arguments of Section
2.1
go through,
provided we consider the shift
\theta
that acts in the
horizontal direction only.
We can then easily obtain that, for the same constant
C=C(p)
corresponding to
the Barak-Erdős graph
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
, we have

L^{*}_{n}/n\to C,\text{ as $n\to\infty$, a.s.\ and in $L^{1}$},

where
C
is the constant for the Barak-Erdős graph

\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
.

The CLT is more interesting.
It involves a process that appears e.g. in
[
88
]
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Consider the random graph
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z}\times I,p)
,
I=\{1,\ldots,M\}

and let
L^{*}_{n}

be the maximum length of all paths in
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\times I
.
Then, with
C,\sigma
as in Theorem
6.10
,

\ell_{n}(t):=\frac{L^{*}_{[nt]}-Cnt}{\sigma\sqrt{\lambda n}},\quad t\geq 0,\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

as a sequence of random elements of
D[0,\infty)
with the local
uniform topology, converges in distribution to the process

Z_{M}(t):=\max_{0=t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq\ldots\leq t_{M}=t}\,\sum_{i=1}^{M}\big(B^{(i)}(t_{i})-B^{(i)}(t_{i-1})\big),

(7.1)

where
B^{(1)},\ldots,B^{(M)}
are independent standard Brownian motions.

Sketch of proof.

If
\mathscr{S}^{(i)}
denotes the set of skeleton points of the restriction of

\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z}\times I,p)
on the line
\mathbb{Z}\times\{i\}
,
we have that the
\mathscr{S}^{(i)}
are independent and all equal in distribution to
the set of skeleton points of the Barak-Erdős graph
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
.
Hence all the
\mathscr{S}^{(i)}
are stationary renewal processes on
\mathbb{Z}

and also aperiodic (in the sense that the greatest common divisor
of the positive integers in the support of the distance between
successive skeleton points is
1
).
Therefore the set
\cap_{i\in I}\mathscr{S}^{(i)}
is also a renewal process
with positive rate
[
74
]
and the even smaller set

\mathscr{S}^{I}=\big\{x\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathscr{S}^{(i)}:\,\text{ for all $i,j\in I$,
$i<j$, there is an edge between $(x,i)$ and $(x,j)$}\big\}

is still a renewal process as it is obtained by thinning.
Moreover,
\mathscr{S}^{I}
is stationary and ergodic (with respect to
\theta)

and has positive rate. Hence
\mathscr{S}^{I}
is an random subset of
\mathbb{Z}
that is
infinite in both directions.
Let
\Gamma_{k}^{I}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, be an enumeration of the elements of
\mathscr{S}^{I}

with
\Gamma_{k}^{I}<\Gamma_{k+1}^{I}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\Gamma^{I}_{0}\leq 0<\Gamma_{1}^{I}
.
Then, as in the Barak-Erdős case (see Lemma
6.1
) is we let

\overrightarrow{G}_{k}
be the induced subgraph of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z}\times I,p)

on
\{(u,i)\in\mathbb{Z}\times I:\,\Gamma_{k}^{I}\leq u\leq\Gamma_{k+1}\}
,
we have that, conditional on
\{0\in\mathscr{S}^{I}\}
,
the
\overrightarrow{G}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, are i.i.d.
Therefore, a maximal-length path on
\{0,\ldots,n\}\times I

is necessarily a path from
(0,1)
to
(n,M)
such that if

0\leq\Gamma_{k}^{I}\leq n
then such a path passes via a vertex whose
horizontal coordinate is
\Gamma_{k}^{I}
; see Figure
8
.

Figure 8
:
A maximal length path on
[0,n]\times I
passes through all
intermediate skeleton points and switches line at some of them.

Using this and breaking
\ell_{n}(t)
as in (
6.13
),
we arrive at the result by using Donsker’s theorem.
Details can be found in
[
39
]
.
∎

7.2
Generalization to partially ordered vertex sets and distance-dependent probabilities

In the above, we can take
I
to be a finite partially ordered set
(I,\preceq)

with a bottom element called
1
and a top element called
M
.
We then equip
\mathbb{Z}\times I
with a strict partial order
\ll

defined by

(u,i)\ll(v,j)\text{ if }(u,i)\neq(v,j)\text{ and }u\leq v,i\preceq j.

A pair of vertices
(u,i)
,
(v,j)
, with
(u,i)\ll(v,j)
,
form an edge directed from
(u,i)
to
(v,j)

with probability
r_{v-u,i,j}
, independently from pair to pair.
As before, we let
L_{(u,i),(v,j)}
the maximum length of paths
from
(u,i)
to
(v,j)
.
A convenient set of assumptions for the probabilities
r_{n,i,j}
is:

\forall i\in I~r_{n,i,i}=:p_{n},\quad 0<p_{1}<1,\quad\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\prod_{m=1}^{n}(1-p_{m})<\infty.

(7.2)

Let as denote by
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z}\times I,(r_{n,i,j}))
the resulting
random graph on
\mathbb{Z}\times I
and let
L^{*}_{0,n}
be the
maximum length of all paths with endpoints in
[0,n]\times I
.

Then Theorem
7.1
remains the same in form,
that is, we normalize
L^{*}_{0,[nt]}
in the same way
and obtain that the normalized sequence of processes
converge in distribution to a process akin to (
7.1
)
but with an additional maximization since the presence of partial
order allows for more flexibility.

To express the limiting process we need the Hasse diagram
\mathbb{H}(I)
of
the partially ordered set
(I,\preceq)
, which is the directed graph
on
I
obtained by declaring there is an edge from
i
to
j

if

\text{$i\preceq j$ and there
is no $k$, distinct from $i$ and $j$, such that $i\preceq k\preceq j$}.

See
[
37
]
.
Let
B^{(i)}
,
i\in I
, be independent standard Brownian motions.
For any path
\iota=(\iota_{0},\ldots,\iota_{r})
in
\mathbb{H}(I)
we write

Z^{(\iota)}(t):=\max_{0=t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq\cdots\leq t_{r}=t}\big\{B^{(\iota_{0})}(t_{1})+[B^{(\iota_{1})}(t_{2})-B^{(\iota_{1})}(t_{1})]+\cdots+[B^{(\iota_{r})}(t_{r})-B^{(\iota_{r})}(t_{r-1})]\big\},

and then let

Z_{I}(t):=\max_{\iota}Z^{(\iota)}(t),

(7.3)

where the maximum is taken over all paths
\iota
in
\mathbb{H}(I)

from
0
to
M
.
Then
Z_{I}
is the limit of the sequence of normalized processes.

7.3
Convergence to the Tracy-Widom distribution

Self-similarity.

It is clear that the process
Z_{I}
of (
7.3
) is not Gaussian,
but is is continuous and self-similar:

(Z_{I}(ct))_{t\geq 0}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}c^{1/2}(Z_{I}(t))_{t\geq 0},

for any
c>0
.
In particular, the process
Z_{M}
of (
7.1
) is
a special case of (
7.3
), when
I=\{1,\ldots,M\}
, has well-known
connection with random matrix theory; see below.

Queueing theory.

Glynn and Whitt
[
55
]
considered an infinite
number single-server FCFS infinite-buffer queues connected in series.
At time
0
there are
M
customers in the first one and none in the others.
Denote by
\sigma_{m,n}
the service time of customer
m
in queue
n
.
It is assumed that the
\sigma_{m,n}
, are i.i.d. random
variables with finite variance.
One quantity of interest in queueing theory is the time
L_{m,n}

that customer
m
departs from queue
n
. For this, we have an obvious recursion,
namely,

L_{m,n}=\max(L_{m-1,n},L_{m,n-1})+\sigma_{m,n},

because if customer
m
finds, upon arrival, queue
n
occupied,
it has to wait until the previous customer departs
from queue
n
at time
L_{m,n-1}
, so
L_{m,n}=L_{m,n-1}+\sigma_{m,n}
;
and if it finds the queue empty then

L_{m,n}=L_{m,n-1}+\sigma_{m,n}
.
We can easily solve the recursion and express it as follows.
Consider
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
as a directed graph where each vertex
(i,j)

has two outgoing edges, one to
(i,j+1)
and one to
(i+1,j)
.
Equip each
(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
with weight
\sigma_{i,j}
.
Let
\pi
be a path in this graph. Then
w(\pi)
is the sum
of the weights of the vertices of this path.
We obtain

L_{m,n}=\max\{w(\pi):\,\pi\text{ is path from $(1,1)$ to $(m,n)$}\},\quad(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}.

Consider now the normalized departure times,

\ell_{m}^{(n)}:=\frac{L_{m,n}-n\mathbb{E}\sigma}{\sqrt{n\operatorname{var}\sigma}},

where
\sigma
has the same law as (any of) the
\sigma_{m,n}
.
Then
[
55
]
show that

\ell_{M}^{(n)}\to Z_{M}(1),\text{ as }n\to\infty,\text{ in distribution},

where
Z_{M}
is as in (
7.1
).
For subsequent work on the symmetry, duality, and other
quantities of interest, see
[
9
]
.

The GUE.

Let
H
be an
M\times M
GUE (Gaussian Unitary Ensemble) random matrix
[
84
]
.
This is a random element of the set of complex Hermitian
M\times M
matrices
such that each diagonal element has standard complex Gaussian distribution
and each off-diagonal element has standard real Gaussian distribution.
(A standard complex Gaussian distribution is the law of
Z=X+\sqrt{-1}Y

where
X,Y
are
i.i.d. Gaussian random variables with
\mathbb{E}X=\mathbb{E}Y=0
,

\mathbb{E}X^{2}=\mathbb{E}Y^{2}=1/2
.)
Moreover, all elements
H_{k,\ell}
,
k\leq\ell
, are independent
(and
H_{\ell,k}=\overline{H}_{k,\ell}
for
k\leq\ell
).
The eigenvalues of
H
are real random variables and the largest of them
is denoted by
\lambda_{M}
.
It is clear that
H
satisfies
H\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}UHU^{-1}
for any unitary
(
U^{-1}=U^{*}
) complex matrix
U
.
It was shown independently by Baryshnikov
[
11
]
and Gravner et al.
[
56
]
, thus answering an open question by
Glynn and Whitt
[
55
]
, that

Z_{M}(1)
satisfies

Z_{M}(1)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\lambda_{M},

(7.4)

thus establishing a connection between Brownian last passage percolation
and random matrix theory.

CLT for the largest GUE eigenvalue.

The law of
\lambda_{m}

satisfies
\lambda_{m}/\sqrt{m}\to 2
.
The CLT for
\lambda_{m}
is

m^{1/6}(\lambda_{m}-2\sqrt{m})\xrightarrow{\text{d}}F_{2},

(7.5)

where the distribution function
F_{2}
a determinantal form:

F_{2}(x)=\det(I-A_{K})_{L^{2}[0,x]},

where
A_{K}
is the operator on
L^{2}[0,x]
with kernel
K
defined

K(x,y)=\frac{\operatorname{Ai}(x)\operatorname{Ai}^{\prime}(y)-\operatorname{Ai}^{\prime}(x)\operatorname{Ai}(y)}{x-y},

and where
\operatorname{Ai}(x)
is the principal Airy function,
defined as the solution
y=Ai(x)
to the linear ODE
y^{\prime\prime}-xy=0

with boundary condtion
y\to 0
as
x\to\infty
.
Fourier-transforming the ODE, at least formally, easily yields
that
\operatorname{Ai}(x):=\pi^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(\tfrac{1}{3}\omega^{3}+\omega x)d\omega=\pi^{-1}\lim_{\Omega\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\Omega}\cos(\tfrac{1}{3}\omega^{3}+\omega x)d\omega
.
Note that
K(x,x)
is defined as a limit when
y\to x
.
The determinant above is to be understood as the Fredholm determinant
of the operator
A_{K}
acting on
L^{2}[0,x]
.
This was established by Tracy and Widom
[
96
]
and the
distribution
F_{2}
is known as the Tracy-Widom law, see also Anderson, Guionnet and Zeitouni
[
7
]
.

Barak-Erdős graph on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
.

Consider the graph
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N},p)
, with
0<p<1
.
We equip
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
with the natural partial order;

(i,j)
is before
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if
i\leq i^{\prime}
,
j\leq j^{\prime}
and
(i.j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
If
(i,j)
is before
(i,j^{\prime})
we put and edge between them with probability
p
,
independently from pair to pair of comparable vertices.
Paths in this graph move in a “northeast” direction (including the north and
the east).
We let
L_{n,m}
be the maximum length of all paths from
(1,1)
to
(n,m)
.
The induced subgraph of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N},p)
on any horizontal
line is a
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{N},p)
.
Consider the
m
-th line and apply Theorem
6.10
.
We obtain

\left(\frac{L_{[nt].m}-Cnt}{\sigma\sqrt{\lambda n}}\right)_{t\geq 0}\xrightarrow{\text{d}}Z_{m},\text{ as }n\to\infty.

On the other hand, by self-similarity and (
7.4
), we have

Z_{m}(t)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\sqrt{t}\lambda_{m},

and
m^{1/6}(\lambda_{m}-2\sqrt{m})\xrightarrow{\text{d}}F_{2}
.
It is then natural to if we can obtain a limit for a normalized

L_{n,m}
when
n
and
m
tend to infinity simultaneously.
To see what kind of scaling we can expect, write (
7.5
) as

m^{1/6}\left(\frac{Z_{m}(t)}{\sqrt{t}}-2\sqrt{m}\right)\xrightarrow{\text{d}}F_{2},\text{ as }m\to\infty.

A statement of the form
X(t,m)\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\text{(d)}}X
,
where the distribution of
X(t,m)
does not depend on the choice of
t>0
,
implies the statement

X(t,m(t))\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{\text{d}}X
, for any function
m(t)

such that
m(t)\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}\infty
. Hence,
upon setting
m=[t^{a}]
, we have

t^{a/6}\bigg(\frac{Z_{[t^{a}]}(t)}{\sqrt{t}}-2\sqrt{t^{a}}\bigg)\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{\text{d}}F_{2}.

Therefore, it is reasonable to guess that an analogous limit theorem
holds for a centered scaled version of the largest
length
L_{n,[n^{a}]}
, namely that

n^{a/6}\bigg(\frac{L_{n,[n^{a}]}-c_{1}n}{c_{2}\sqrt{n}}-2\sqrt{n^{a}}\bigg)\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{\text{d}}F_{2},

(7.6)

where
c_{1},c_{2}
are appropriate constants.
Indeed, (
7.6
) holds, with
c_{1}=C=C(p)
and
c_{2}=\lambda\sigma^{2}

and
a
sufficiently small.
This was proved in
[
72
, Theorem 6.1]

for the more general case of graphs where the edge probabilities
may not be constant, as in Section
6
. The smallness of
a

depends on a condition that involves the edge probabilities.

That paper used the idea of strong coupling with Brownian motions
(Komlós-Major-Tusnády
[
70
,
71
, Theorem 4]
),
as in
[
17
]
, a paper dealing with last passage percolation
on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
with random weights on the vertices.
The difficulty in the proof of the main theorem in
[
72
]
is that
if we consider the intersection of the sets of skeleton points corresponding
to each line then their intersection is empty.

We also refer the reader to the seminal paper of Johansson
[
63
]

for last passage percolation on a lattice (with i.i.d. exponential weights
on vertices).
There are also exciting connections with processes fundamental
to modern probability theory and mathematical physics, such as the
Kardar-Parizi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class.
We refer to the recent works by Dauvergne
et al

[
35
,
36
]
on
the convergence of the Brownian last passage percolation
to the central object in the KPZ class, namely the
directed landscape
.

8
Weighted Barak-Erdős graphs

We now sketch results on last passage percolation on the Barak-Erdős
graph with random weights on its edges; we refer
to
[
49
]
and
[
45
]
for details.
Recall that existence
of an edge
(i,j)
is encoded by a random variable
\alpha_{i,j}

that takes value
1
with probability
p
or
-\infty

with probability
1-p
.
Let
u
be a positive random variable with
distribution function
F(x)=\mathbb{P}(u\leq x)
,
and let
u_{i,j}
,
i<j
, be a collection of i.i.d. copies of
u
.
If
\alpha_{i,j}=1
then edge
(i,j)
exists and has weight
u_{i,j}
.
We now have a weighted random graph that we will denote as
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p,u)
.
A path from
i
to
j
of length
\ell
and weight
w
is
a sequence
(i=i_{0}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell-1}<i_{\ell}=j)

such that
\alpha_{i_{0},i_{1}}=\cdots=\alpha_{i_{\ell-1},i_{\ell}}=1

and
u_{i_{0},i_{1}}+\cdots+u_{i_{\ell-1},i_{\ell}}=w
.
A
geodesic
from
i
to
j
is a maximum weight path from
i
to
j
.

Different phenomena appear depending on whether
\mathbb{E}u^{2}
is finite
or not.

8.1
Finite variance weights

Assume that
\mathbb{E}u^{2}<\infty
.
We study

W_{i,j}:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=i_{0}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}=j\\
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}u_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}\right)^{+},

the maximum weight of all paths from
i
and
j
.
If we further define the larger quantity

\widetilde{W}_{i,j}:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq i_{0}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}\leq j\\
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}u_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}\right)^{+}
,
the maximum weight of all paths with endpoints between
i
and
j

we obtain the subadditive inequality

\widetilde{W}_{i,k}\leq\widetilde{W}_{i,j}+\widetilde{W}_{j,k}+\max_{i\leq x\leq j\leq y\leq k}u_{x,y}.

We can then see that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{W}_{0,n}/n
exists
iff the expectation of the latter maximum is finite
which requires that the second moment of
u
be finite.
Hence
\mathbb{E}u^{2}<\infty
is necessary and sufficient
for the above limit to exist.

To understand this, and to prepare the ground for the central
limit theorem, we consider two random subsets of the integers.
The first is the usual skeleton set
\mathscr{S}
and the second is the set
\mathscr{R}_{c}
of

c
-renewal points
where
c
is a positive constant.
To define this, we first define the events

\displaystyle A_{i}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{W_{i,i+n}>cn\text{ for all }n\geq 1\},

\displaystyle A_{i}^{-}

\displaystyle=\{W_{i-n,i}>cn\text{ for all }n\geq 1\},

\displaystyle A_{i}^{-+}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha_{i-m,i+n}u_{i-m,i+n}<c(m+n)\text{ for all }m,n\geq 1\},

and then let

\mathscr{R}_{c}=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:\,A_{i}^{+}\cap A_{i}^{-}\cap A_{i}^{-+}\text{ occurs}\}.

Clearly,
\mathscr{R}_{c}
is a stationary and ergodic random set with density

\mu(c,p)=\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{+}\cap A_{0}^{-}\cap A_{0}^{-+}).

Since

\mathscr{R}_{c}\subset\mathscr{S},

we have
\mu(c,p)\leq\lambda(p)
, where
\lambda(p)
is the
density of
\mathscr{S}
; see (
2.9
) and Remark
2.9
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}u^{2}<\infty
and
0<c<(\mathbb{E}u)(\mathbb{E}W_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}})
.
Then
\mu(c,p)>0
.
Moreover,
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p,u)
regenerates over
\mathscr{R}_{c}
.

Sketch of proof.

We first observe that
\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{+})=\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{-})
.
If we choose
0<c<(\mathbb{E}u)(\mathbb{E}W_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}})

then
\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{+})>0
.
The finiteness of
\mathbb{E}u^{2}

implies the positivity of
\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{-+})
.
It can be shown that
A_{0}^{+},A_{0}^{-},A_{0}^{-+}
are independent
and so
\mu(c,p)=\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{+})\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{-})\mathbb{P}(A_{0}^{-+})>0
.
For details see

[
45
, Lemma 2]
.
For the last assertion see
[
45
, Lemma 3]
.
∎

This lemma is responsible for the law of large numbers:

Theorem 8.2
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}u^{2}<\infty
and
0<p\leq 1
.
Then there is a constant
C
depending on

p
and the law of
u
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{W_{0,n}}{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\widetilde{W}_{0,n}}{n}=C\text{ a.s. and in $L^{1}$}.

We remark that the equality of the two limits is because of the
existence of the
c
-renewal points that have positive density.
The CLT holds provided that the third moment of
u
is finite:

Theorem 8.3
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}u^{3}<\infty
and
0<p\leq 1
.
Then the sequence of processes

\left\{\frac{W_{0,[nt]}-Cnt}{\sqrt{\lambda n}},\,t\geq 0\right\}

converges in distribution, as
n\to\infty
, to a
zero mean Brownian motion.

Theorems
8.2
and
8.3
may be complemented by a result
describing the behavior of the weight of the heaviest edge on
a geodesic path. Assuming that
F
is continuous ensures that
there is a unique geodesic path from
0
to
n
. Then we can define

h_{n}:=\text{ maximum weight of all edges on the geodesic path from
$0$ to $n$.}

We also assume that the edge weight
u
is regularly varying
with index
s
, in the sense that

\frac{1-F(tx)}{1-F(x)}\to t^{-s},\text{ as }x\to\infty,\text{ for every }t>0.

(8.1)

Of course,
s>2
is needed in order that
\mathbb{E}u^{2}
be finite.
When
2<s<3
, one can deduce
that the fluctuations of
W_{0,n}
are of order larger than

\sqrt{n}
, and so the central limit theorem cannot be extended to this
case.

Theorem 8.4
.

Let the edge weight
u
be a continuous random variable
that is also regularly varying with index
s>2

in the sense of (
8.1
).
Then we have

\frac{\log h_{n}}{\log n}\rightarrow\frac{1}{s-1}\text{ in probability as }n\rightarrow\infty.

(8.2)

In particular, if
2<s<3
then

\frac{\operatorname{var}W_{0,n}}{n}\rightarrow\infty,

and a central limit theorem such as that in Theorem
8.3
cannot hold.

The proof of this can be found in
[
49
]
.

8.2
Infinite variance weights

Assume now that
\mathbb{E}u^{2}=\infty
.
Under this condition,
W_{0,n}
grows faster than linearly.
This can be seen by considering the contribution of the single heaviest
edge in
[0,n]
, and noting that
the expectation of the maximum of

n^{2}
i.i.d. random variables with infinite variance
has expectation that grows faster than
n
.
Since
W_{0,n}
is at least as large as the weight of this single edge,
we have that
\mathbb{E}W_{0,n}/n\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and
from Kingman’s subadditive ergodic
theorem we can conclude that in fact
W_{0,n}/n\to\infty
a.s.

As before, we assume that
u
is a continuous random variable
such that the regular variation condition (
8.1
) holds.
We need
s<2
in order that
\mathbb{E}u^{2}
be infinite.

New phenomena occur in the infinite variance case. In order to
describe them succinctly and avoid technicalities, we shall
further assume that
p=1
. That is, we only present results
for the
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},1,u)
case.

Finite model

Let
\overrightarrow{G}_{n}
be a graph on
V_{n}=\left\{0,\frac{1}{n},\ldots,\frac{n-1}{n},1\right\}
,
edges
E_{n}=\{(i/n,j/n):\,0\leq i<j\leq n\}
,
and weights
u^{(n)}_{e}
,
e\in E_{n}
, that are i.i.d. copies of
u
.
We can think of a path
\pi
of
\overrightarrow{G}_{n}
as a collection
of edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
where the ending point of
e_{i}
is the
starting point of
e_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i<\ell
.
Let
\Pi_{n}
be the set of all paths in
\overrightarrow{G}_{n}
from
0
to
n

(a set of size
2^{n-1}
).
The maximum weight of all paths in
\Pi_{n}
can be written as

W_{0,n}=\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{n}}\sum_{e\in\pi}u^{(n)}_{e}.

The latter maximum will not increase if we throw in all
admissible
subsets
of the set of edges
E_{n}
, where we say that a set
A\subset E_{n}
is admissible
if every pair of elements of
A
are non-overlapping edges
in the sense that the endpoints of one are
\leq
the endpoints of the other.
If we let
\mathcal{C}_{n}
be the set of all admissible sets of edges then

W_{0,n}=\max_{\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}\sum_{e\in A}u^{(n)}_{e}.

(8.3)

We next introduce another way to construct
\overrightarrow{G}_{n}
.
This second construction can be used to define
a corresponding model on a continuous set of vertices
with an appropriately defined maximum path weight
W

in such a way that a scaled version of
W_{0,n}

converges to
W
in distribution.
Note that
E_{n}
has size

N=N_{n}=\binom{n+1}{2}.

Let
E_{n}=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}\}
be an enumeration of the edges.
Let
M_{e_{1}}^{(n)},M_{e_{2}}^{(n)},\ldots,M_{e_{N}}^{(n)}
be the order statistics
of the
u^{(n)}_{e_{1}},u^{(n)}_{e_{2}},\ldots,u^{(n)}_{e_{N}}

That is,

\{M_{e_{1}}^{(n)},M_{e_{2}}^{(n)},\ldots,M_{e_{N}}^{(n)}\}=\{u^{(n)}_{e_{1}},u^{(n)}_{e_{2}},\ldots,u^{(n)}_{e_{N}}\}

(as sets) and
M_{e_{1}}^{(n)}>M_{e_{2}}^{(n)}>\cdots>M_{e_{N}}^{(n)}
.

Let
Y_{1}^{(n)},Y_{2}^{(n)},\ldots,Y_{N}^{(n)}
be a random
ordering of
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}\}
chosen uniformly from all the
N!

possibilities. Assign weight
M_{e_{i}}^{(n)}
to
Y_{i}^{(n)}
.
We then have

\mathcal{C}_{n}=\left\{A\subset\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}\right\}:\text{ for every pair $\{e_{i},e_{j}\}\subset A$ the $Y_{e_{i}}^{(n)}$, $Y_{e_{j}}^{(n)}$
are non-overlapping}\right\}

(8.4)

and

W_{0,n}=\max_{A\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}\sum_{e_{i}\in A}M_{e_{i}}^{(n)}

(8.5)

which is equivalent (the same in distribution)
to the previous definition of
W_{0,n}
in (
8.3
).

Infinite model

We next define a random weighted graph
\overrightarrow{G}
on countably infinite random set
of vertices.
Let
W_{1},W_{2},\ldots
be a sequence of i.i.d. exponential random variables with mean 1 each.
Set

M_{k}=(W_{1}+\cdots+W_{k})^{-1/s},\quad k=1,2,\ldots.

Let
U_{1},U_{2},\ldots
and
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots
be
two sequences of i.i.d. uniform random variables in
[0,1]
.
We further assume that
\{U_{i}\}
,
\{V_{i}\}
,
\{W_{i}\}
are independent.
The edges of
\overrightarrow{G}
are taken to be

Y_{i}=(\min(U_{i},V_{i}),\,\max(U_{i},V_{i})),\quad i=1,2,\ldots.

The
i
-th largest weight
M_{i}
will be attached
to the
i
-th edge
Y_{i}
. In analogy to (
8.4
) we define

\mathcal{C}=\left\{A\subset\left\{1,2,\ldots\right\}:Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}=\varnothing\text{ for all pairs }\{i,j\}\subset A\right\}.

In analogy to (
8.5
) we let

W=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{i\in A}M_{i}.

(8.6)

A priori the random variable
W
could be infinite, but
Theorem
8.5
below guarantees that it is almost surely finite.

Convergence results

The intuition behind the approximation of the finite model by the infinite one
is the following pair of convergence results.
First, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\left(Y_{1}^{(n)},Y_{2}^{(n)},\ldots,Y_{k}^{(n)}\right)\xrightarrow{\text{\rm d}}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{k})

(8.7)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where we use the product topology on
([0,1]^{2})^{k}
.

Following
[
82
,
57
]
,
let
b_{n}=a_{N_{n}}=F^{(-1)}\left(1-\frac{1}{N_{n}}\right)

and put

\widetilde{M}_{i}^{(n)}=\frac{M_{i}^{(n)}}{b_{n}}.

(As an example, if

F(x)=1-x^{-s}
for
x\geq 1
, then
b_{n}
grows like
n^{2/s}
.
More generally, under assumption (
8.1
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log b_{n}}{\log n}=2/s
).
Then from classical results
in extreme value theory we have for any

k\in\mathbb{N}
that

\left(\widetilde{M}_{1}^{(n)},\widetilde{M}_{2}^{(n)},\ldots,\widetilde{M}_{k}^{(n)}\right)\xrightarrow{\text{\rm d}}\left(M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{k}\right)\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

(8.8)

In this way both the locations and weights of the heaviest edges
(i.e. edges with heaviest weights)
in the discrete model above are approximated
by their equivalents in the continuous model.
It is shown in
[
49
]
that the heaviest edges are the ones that bring dominant contribution to the maximum weight.
The precise convergence result is as follows.

Theorem 8.5
.

The random variable
W
in (
8.6
) is almost surely finite.
If (
8.1
) holds with
s\in(0,2)
, then

\frac{W_{0,n}}{b_{n}}\rightarrow W
in distribution as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

For
n
large, the heaviest edge in the geodesic has length on the order of
n
.
This is in contrast to the behavior in the case
\mathbb{E}u^{2}<\infty
,
where the important contribution to the maximal weight is given by edges of a
lighter order, see e.g. Theorem
8.3
above.

9
Analytic properties of charged graphs

We now turn to some results concerning the behavior of the last
passage percolation constant
C
of the Barak-Erdős graph
as a function of edge weights. The problem is, in general, hard,
so we consider here the simple model introduced in
[
47
]
.
The next section will deal with a more general model.

A charged graph is a graph with possibly negative weights on its edges.
We are interested in last passage percolation on random directed
charged graphs. The charge of a path is the sum of the charges of its edges.
We are interested in the maximum charge of all paths.
If all charges are negative then the maximum of negative quantities is the
negative of a minimum of positive quantities, so the problem becomes that
of first passage percolation,
In view of this, we shall assume that some charges are nonnegative.

For the models of this and the next section we assume that the support of the
charge distribution is not a subset of
(-\infty,0)
.
It will then turn out that the last passage percolation constant is positive,
so it matters little if we take positive part of the charge of a path
before maximization.

9.1
The two-weights model

Let
x
be a real number, possibly negative (which can be thought of
as a penalty).
To each pair
(i,j)
of positive integers with
i<j
we assign weight
or, rather, charge (since
x
is allowed to be negative)

w^{x}_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ with probability }p\\
x,&\text{ with probability }1-p\end{cases}.

Let
\Pi_{i,j}
be the set of
strictly increasing finite sequences of integers,

i_{0}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}
such that
i_{0}=i
and
i_{\ell}=j
.
An element
\pi\in\Pi_{i,j}
is a finite path in the complete directed graph
on
\mathbb{Z}
(that is, a graph such that every
(i,j)
with
i<j
is an
edge directed from
i
to
j
).
So
\Pi_{i,j}
is a deterministic set of size
2^{j-i}-1
.
Define

w^{x}(\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}w^{x}_{i_{k-1},i_{k}},\quad\text{ if }\pi=(i_{0},\ldots,i_{\ell})\in\Pi_{i,j},

and call
w^{x}(\pi)
the charge or weight of
\pi
.
We are interested in

W^{x}_{i,j}=\max_{\pi=(i_{0},\ldots,i_{\ell})\in\Pi_{i,j}}~\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}w^{x}_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}.

(9.1)

We will use the notation
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p,x)
to denote the directed
charged random graph on
\mathbb{Z}

that contains as edges all
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
with
i<j

and which has with i.i.d. edge charges distributed as
w^{x}_{i,j}
.
We study the asymptotic growth rate
C(p,x)

of
W^{x}_{i,j}
; see (
9.2
) below.
The Barak-Erdős graph is still denoted by
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)

and, as usual,
C(p)
is the maximal path growth rate.
The parameter
p
is fixed throughout this section.
We shall be interested in the behavior of the model when
x
varies.

We may extend the model by letting
x
range in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\}
.
The case
x=+\infty
is uninteresting as being trivial.
The case
x=-\infty
formally corresponds to the Barak-Erdős graph,
the reason being as follows.
The charge of
i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}
equals
-\infty
iff

w^{x}_{i_{k},i_{k+1}}=-\infty
for some
k
, and this is equivalent to

i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}
not being a path in
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
.
Hence
(W^{-\infty}_{i,j})^{+}=L^{\mathsf{L},\mathsf{R}}_{i,j}

and
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p,-\infty)=\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
.

An alternative way to think of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p,x)
is by letting all
edges of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
be
blue
and all non-edges be
red
.
Blue edges have charge 1; red edges have charge
x
.

Theorem 9.1
.

For
-\infty<x<\infty
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{W^{x}_{0,n}}{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{(W^{x}_{0,n})^{+}}{n}=\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\mathbb{E}(W^{x}_{0,n})^{+}}{n}=:C(p,x),

(9.2)

a.s. and in
L^{1}
.

The constant
C(p,x)
is defined through this theorem.
The theorem is proved by using ergodic arguments and Kingman’s theorem,
thanks to the superadditive inequality

W^{x}_{i,k}\geq W^{x}_{i,j}+W^{x}_{j,k},\quad i<j<k.

Further properties of
C(p,x)
are in Theorem
9.2
below.
We shall use the following notations.

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}_{C}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\,C(p,\cdot)\text{ is not differentiable at }x\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{Q}\setminus\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Y}

\displaystyle:=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}:\,q<0\}\cup\{0\}\cup\{\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{3},\ldots\}\cup\{2,3,\ldots\}

Theorem 9.2
.

(i)

Scaling.
C(p,x)=xC(1-p,x)
, for all
x>0
.

(ii)

Continuity at
-\infty
.
\lim_{x\to-\infty}C(p,x)=C(p)
.

(iii)

Convexity.
C(p,x)
is increasing convex over
x\in\mathbb{R}
and strictly positive.

(iv)

Asymptotic growth.
\lim_{x\to\infty}C(p,x)/x=C(1-p,0)=\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}p^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)}\right)^{-1}
.

(v)

Nondifferentiability.
\mathbb{X}_{C}=\mathbb{Y}
.

The proof of (v) is the most complex and shall only be sketched below.
Property (i) follows from by comparing
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p,x)
with

\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},1-p,1/x)
.
Property (iii) follows from the fact that
W^{x}_{i,j}
is
an increasing convex function of
x
.
Strict positivity is due to
C(p,x)>C(p)>0
for all
x
.
The first part of Property (iv) is a consequence of continuity of the convex function
and the scaling property (i).
The last formula of (iv) is due to Dutta
[
41
]
.

To deal with (v), we start by further elaborating on
skeleton points.

9.2
Further structure of inter-skeleton pieces

As usual,
[m,n]
stands for the set of all integers
k
with
m\leq k\leq n
.
That the set
\mathscr{S}
of skeleton points of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
splits

\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p,x)
into independent parts is not a surprise;
see Lemma
6.1
.
Hence, if
(i_{0},\ldots,i_{\ell})
achieves the maximum
in the right-hand side of the definition (
9.1
) for
W^{x}_{i,j}
,
then
\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
contains all elements of
\mathscr{S}\cap[i,j]
.

Enumerate the elements
\Gamma_{k}
of
\mathscr{S}
as in (
6.2
).
Let
\overrightarrow{G}(I,p)
be the Barak-Erdős graph on the (possibly
random) set of vertices
I\subset\mathbb{Z}
.

Fix a positive integer
n
.
Consider the event
A^{+}_{0,n}
that there is a path in
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)

from
0
to every vertex in
(1,n]

and the event
A^{-}_{0,n}
that there is a path from
every vertex in
[0,n-1)
to
n
; see (
6.4
).
In addition, let

F_{0,n}:=\big\{\text{for all $0<j<n$ there is $0<i<n$
with no path from $\min(i,j)$ to $\max(i,j)$}\big\},

and then set

H_{0,n}:=A^{+}_{0,n}\cap A^{-}_{0,n}\cap F_{0,n}.

The following is an expression for the distribution of

\overrightarrow{G}([\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}],p)
conditional on
\{\Gamma_{0}=0\}
.

Proposition 9.3
.

For any nonnegative
deterministic functional
\varphi\big(\overrightarrow{G}([\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}],p\big)

of
\overrightarrow{G}([\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}],p)
we have

\mathbb{E}\left\{\varphi\big(\overrightarrow{G}([\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}],p\big)\big|\Gamma_{0}=0\right\}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\{\varphi\big(\overrightarrow{G}([0,n],p\big);H_{0,n}\right\},

(9.3)

In particular,

\mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0}=n|\Gamma_{0}=0)=\mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}=n)=\mathbb{P}(H_{0,n}).

(9.4)

Furthermore,

C(p,x)=\lambda\mathbb{E}W^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}=\lambda\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(W^{x}_{0,n};H_{0,n}),

(9.5)

where
\lambda=\lambda(p)
is as in (
2.9
).

For a proof see
[
47
]
. The intuition should be clear
because, if, say,
A^{+}_{0,n}\cap A^{-}_{0,n}
holds
(in which case both
0
and
n
are in
\mathscr{S}
)
but
F_{0,n}
fails, then there is an element of
\mathscr{S}
strictly
between
0
and
n
.
The first equality of (
9.5
) is by standard renewal theory.
The second follows from (
9.3
).

Remark 9.4
.

From (
9.4
) we can obtain some values of the distribution of

\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}
:

\mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}=n)=\begin{cases}p,&\text{ if }n=1\\
0,&\text{ if }n=2\\
p^{4}(1-p),&\text{ if }n=3\\
p^{7}(1-p)^{3}+3p^{5}(1-p)^{2},&\text{ if }n=4\end{cases}

The smallest value of
\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}
is
1
.
Suppose that
\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}\geq 2
.
Note that
\overrightarrow{G}([\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}],p)
must necessarily
have the edges
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{1}+1)
and
(\Gamma_{2}-1,\Gamma_{2})
.
If
\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}
were allowed to take value
2

then
\Gamma_{1}+1=\Gamma_{2}-1
would have been a skeleton point
strictly between
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, which is impossible.
This explains why
\mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{1}=2)=0
.

9.3
Criticality and nondifferentiability

Let
\pi=(i_{0},\ldots,i_{\ell})
be a finite strictly increasing
sequence of integers.
Let
N(\pi)
be the number of
(i_{k-1},i_{k})
,
k=1,\ldots,\ell

that are also edges of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
.
Thus,
N(\pi)
is the number of blue edges of
\pi
.
Similarly,
\overline{N}(\pi)
is the number of red edges.
Then

w^{x}(\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}w^{x}_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}=N(\pi)+x\overline{N}(\pi).

Define

\Pi^{x}_{i,j}:=\{\pi\in\Pi_{i,j}:\,w^{x}(\pi)=W^{x}_{i,j}\},

a random set of paths with maximal charge.
Let
D^{+}
denote right derivative with respect to
x
;
similarly
D^{-}
for left derivative.
Using (
9.5
) and the dominated convergence theorem we obtain

\displaystyle D^{+}C(p,x)

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathbb{E}D^{+}W^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}=\lambda\mathbb{E}\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}}\overline{N}(\pi),

\displaystyle D^{-}C(p,x)

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathbb{E}D^{-}W^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}=\lambda\mathbb{E}\min_{\pi\in\Pi^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}}\overline{N}(\pi).

Remark 9.5
.

To justify the last equalities in these two displays we simply observe that,
by (
9.1
),
W^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}
is the maximum of
the affine functions
x\mapsto w^{x}(\pi)=N(\pi)+x\overline{N}(\pi)
,
over all
\pi\in\Pi_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}
and there are finitely many such
\pi
.
But if we fix
x
, we may further restrict the maximum over all

\pi\in\Pi^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}
.
More generally, if
T
is an index set, and if
a_{t},b_{t}
,
t\in T
, are
real numbers, then the function
f(x)=\max_{t}(a_{t}+b_{t}x)

is convex and thus has right and left derivatives for each
x
,
given by
D^{+}f(x)=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}(1/h)(f(x+h)-f(x))=\sup_{t\in T^{x}}b_{t}
, where
T^{x}=\{t\in T:a_{t}+b_{t}x=f(x)\}
;
and similarly for
D^{-}f(x)
.
In the language of convex analysis
[
91
]
,
we say that set of affine functions

x\mapsto a_{t}+b_{t}x
,
t\in T^{x}
, are supporting lines of the function

f
at the point
x
(the remaining ones are irrelevant) and these are the
only ones responsible for the right and left derivatives of
f
at
x
.

Definition 9.6

(the sets
\mathfrak{G}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{H}_{n}
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{n}
be the set of all directed graphs on
[0,n]
,
that is, graphs whose edge directions
compatible with the natural ordering of integers.
Let
\mathfrak{H}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{G}_{n}
contain all
G\in\mathfrak{G}_{n}
such that,
for all
0<j<n
,

(a)

there is a path in
G
from
0
to
n
containing
j
;

(b)

for some
i\neq j
there is no path in
G
from
\min(i,j)

to
\max(i,j)

For any path
\pi
in a graph
G
we let
N_{G}(\pi)
be the number
of edges of
\pi
that are also edges of
G
and we let

\overline{N}_{G}(\pi)
the number of edges of
\pi
that are
not edges of
G
.

Definition 9.7

(criticality)
.

We say that
x\in\mathbb{R}
is
critical
if there is a positive integer
n

and and a graph
G\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}
possessing two paths
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}

such that

(a)

N_{G}(\pi_{1})+x\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{1})=N_{G}(\pi_{2})+x\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{2})=\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{0,n}}(N_{G}(\pi)+x\overline{N}_{G}(\pi))

(b)

\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{1})\neq\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{2})
.

Criticality is a property of real numbers.
We let

\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x\text{ is critical}\}.

Using Proposition
9.3
we can prove

Theorem 9.8
.

\mathbb{X}_{C}=\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}.

In view of this, Theorem
9.2
(v) is equivalent to

\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}=\mathbb{Y},

(9.6)

which is a deterministic problem of graph-theoretic/combinatorial nature.
Proving this is rather complicated. We shall sketch some aspects of it
below.

9.4
\mathbb{Y}\subset\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
G\in\mathfrak{G}_{n}
and
\pi\in\Pi_{0,n}
.
Think of the common edges of
\pi
and
G
as blue and the remaining
edges of
\pi
as red. There are
N_{G}(\pi)
blue and
\overline{N}_{G}(\pi)

red edges. The path
\pi
is called
(x,G)
-maximal
if it
achieves the maximum in

\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{0,n}}(N_{G}(\pi)+x\overline{N}_{G}(\pi))
.

Showing that
x\in X_{\text{\rm crit}}
one proceeds by constructing a
G\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}

possessing at least two
(x,G)
-maximal paths with
different number of red edges.
Figure
9
summarizes how this is done for
each element
x
of
\mathbb{Y}
, except
x=2,3,\ldots
,
since if we know that
1/k\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
(
k=2,3,\ldots
)
then we also know that
k\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
by the scaling property.
For example, Figure
9
(a) shows that
0\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
.
Indeed, consider the paths
\pi_{1}=(0,1,3)
and
\pi_{2}=(0,1,2,3)
.
They both have weight
2
(which is maximal), however, the first
has no red edges but the second has one red edge.
This means that
0
satisfies the definition of criticality.

\bullet
0
\bullet
1
\bullet
2
\bullet
3

(a)

Each red edge (i.e. each non-edge) has charge
0
.
This graph shows that
0\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
.

\bullet
0
\bullet
1
\bullet
k
\bullet
k+1
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

(b)

Let
k\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
. Each non-edge has charge
1/k
. This graph shows that
1/k\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
.

\bullet
0
\bullet
1
\bullet
\ell+1
\bullet
\ell+2
\bullet
n-1
\bullet
n:=2\ell+3
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

(c)

Let
-\ell\in\{-1,-2,\ldots\}
.
Each non-edge has charge
-1/\ell
.
This graph shows that
-\ell\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
.

0
a_{0}
a_{1}
a_{t-1}
a_{t}
n
0
n
0
n
0
n

(d)

Let
x<0
, rational, but not an integer. Each non-edge has charge
x
.
Write
x=-\ell+(s/t)
,
\ell,s,t\in\mathbb{N}
,
t>1
,
\gcd(s,t)=1
,
s<t
.

Figure 9
:
Graphs exhibiting that
\mathbb{Y}\subset\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
.

The most complex case is that of the last line of Figure
9
.
We explain the construction of the graph.
We first need the following number-theoretic lemma that can be thought of as
placing
n
balls in
N
bins “as uniformly as possible”.
Let
\lfloor x\rfloor
,
\lceil x\rceil
be the lower, respectively upper,
parts of
x
.

Lemma 9.9

(Corollary to Sturm’s lemma
[
75
]
)
.

Let
N,n
be positive integers,
N\geq n
. Then
there exists a unique finite sequence
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N})
of elements
of
\{0,1\}
such that, for all
0\leq i<j\leq N
,
the following hold:

\displaystyle\sum_{i<k\leq j}v_{k}\in\bigg\{\bigg\lfloor(j-i)\frac{n}{N}\bigg\rfloor,\,\bigg\lceil(j-i)\frac{n}{N}\bigg\rceil\bigg\},\quad\sum_{i<k\leq N}v_{k}=\bigg\lfloor(N-i)\frac{n}{N}\bigg\rfloor,\quad v_{1}=1.

We say that the sequence
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N})
defined
through this lemma is the
(N,n)
–balanced
sequence.
For example, with
N=7

n=4
we find

(v_{1},\ldots,v_{7})=(1,1,0,1,0,1,0)

Next, given
x<0
,
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
, write

x=-\ell+\frac{s}{t},

uniquely, where
\ell,s,t
are positive integers,
t>1
,

\gcd(s,t)=1
,
s<t
,
and let
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{t})
be the
(t,t-s)
–balanced sequence.
Construct a graph
G
on
[0,n=3m]
where

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle:=t(\ell+3)-(s+1),

by letting the first and last parts consist of edges joining consecutive
integers and making the middle part as shown in the figure.
The vetices
a_{0},\ldots,a_{t}
are defined by

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle:=m,

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle:=a_{0}+(\ell+1)+v_{1},

\displaystyle a_{j}

\displaystyle:=a_{j-1}+(\ell+2)+v_{j},\quad j=2,\ldots,t.

Finally,
0
is connected by an edge to some vertices as show in
the figure and some vertices are connected to
n
directly.
For example, with
x=-11/7
we have
x=-2+(3/7)
,
that is,
\ell=2
,
s=3
,
t=7
,
m=31
,
n=93
.
The
(7,4)
balanced sequence is
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{7})=(1,1,0,1,0,1,0)
.
We also have

a_{0}=31
,

a_{1}-a_{0}=\ell+1+v_{1}=4
,

a_{2}-a_{1}=\ell+2+v_{2}=5
,

a_{3}-a_{2}=4
,

a_{4}-a_{3}=5
,

a_{5}-a_{4}=4
,

a_{6}-a_{5}=5
,

a_{7}-a_{6}=4
.
That this graph belongs to
\mathfrak{H}_{n}
is not obvious, neither it is obvious
that
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
. The details are omitted.

9.5
\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}\subset\mathbb{Y}

We will show that
\mathbb{Y}^{c}\subset\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}^{c}
.
Note that all irrationals are elements of
\mathbb{Y}^{c}
.

First assume that
x\in\mathbb{Y}^{c}
is irrational.
Since, by Proposition
9.8
,
\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}=\mathbb{X}_{C}
,
we show that
x\in\mathbb{X}_{C}^{c}
.
The set of points at which
x\mapsto W^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}
fails
to be differentiable
is included in the set of points
x
for which there are two paths
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}

from
\Gamma_{1}
to
\Gamma_{2}

such that
w^{x}(\pi_{1})=w^{x}(\pi_{2})
with

\overline{N}(\pi_{1})\neq\overline{N}(\pi_{2})
.
This implies that
(\overline{N}(\pi_{2})-\overline{N}(\pi_{1}))x=N(\pi_{1})-N(\pi_{2})
, which means that
x
is rational.
Hence
W^{x}_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}
is differentiable at rational points
x

and, by the dominated convergence theorem, the same is true for
C(p.x)
.
We thus proved that if
x
is irrational then
x\in\mathbb{X}_{C}^{c}
.

Next let
x\in\mathbb{Y}^{c}
but not irrational.
Showing that
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}^{c}
one proceeds by showing that
for every
n\geq 3
and every
G\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}
there is a unique

(x,G)
-maximal path or that every
(x,G)
-maximal path has the same

\overline{N}_{G}(\pi)
(=number of red edges).

If
x=1
then
every edge and every non-edge of a graph
G\in\mathfrak{G}_{n}
has charge
1
.
Hence the maximum length of all paths is
n
and this is achieved
exactly one path, the path
(0,1,2,\ldots,n)
.
So
1\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}^{c}
.

We know that negative rationals and the number
0
are critical.
So assume that
x>0
. We know that
1
is not critical.
So assume that
x\in(0,1)\cup(1,\infty)
.
By the scaling property, we only need to consider
x\in(0,1)
.
Since
1/2,1/3,\ldots
are critical, we assume that
x\in(0,1)\setminus\{1/2,1/3,\ldots\}
.

Proposition 9.10
.

Every
0<x<1
that is not the reciprocal of an integer is not critical.

The proof of this is rather lengthy and relies on special properties
of maximal paths that can be found in Lemmas 4.5, 4.6, 4.7, 4.8, 4.9 and
4.10 of
[
47
]
.
To state them, we adopt some terminology.
Call an edge
short
if
its endpoints are successive integers; otherwise, call it
long
.
Say that edge
e=(i,j)
is
nested
in
e^{\prime}=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

if
i^{\prime}\leq i<j\leq j^{\prime}
and
e\neq e^{\prime}
.
Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{0,n}
.
We say that the interval
[i,j]\subset[0,n]
is
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
-special

if the set of vertices
k\in[i,j]
that belong to both
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}

consists of
i
and
j
only.

Lemma 9.11
.

If
0<x<2
then every maximal path contains all short blue edges.

Lemma 9.12
.

If
0<x<2

then every long edge of a maximal path
\pi
is blue
(in other words, every red edge of
\pi
must be short).

Lemma 9.13
.

If
x>0
then no blue edge of a maximal path can be nested in a
different blue edge of another
maximal path.

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
0<x<2
. Then for every pair
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{0,n}
of maximal paths such that

\overline{N}_{G}(\pi)\neq\overline{N}_{G}(\pi^{\prime})

there is a
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
-special interval
I
such that

\overline{N}_{G}(\pi|_{I})\neq\overline{N}_{G}(\pi^{\prime}|_{I})

and

N_{G}(\pi|_{I})-N_{G}(\pi^{\prime}|_{I})\in\{-1,1\}.

The proof of the lemmas are omitted, but we give the proof of
Proposition
9.10
:

Proof of Proposition
9.10
.

We prove the contrapositive: if
0<x<1
is critical then
x=1/m
for
some integer
m
.
So suppose that
x
is critical and
0<x<1
.
Then there is
n\geq 3
and
G\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}

(edges of
G
are called blue and non-edges red) and two maximal paths
\pi_{1}
,

\pi_{2}
with different number of red edges:
\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{1})\neq\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{2})
.
By the Lemma
9.14
, there is a
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
-special interval
[i,j]

such that
N_{G}(\pi_{1}|_{I})-N_{G}(\pi_{2}|_{I})\in\{1,-1\}
.
Since

0=w^{x}_{G}(\pi_{1}|_{I})-w^{x}_{G}(\pi_{2}|_{I})=(\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{1}|_{I})-\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{2}|_{I}))x+(N_{G}(\pi_{1}|_{I})-N_{G}(\pi_{2}|_{I})),

it follows that

x=\frac{1}{|\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{1}|{[i,j]})-\overline{N}_{G}(\pi_{2}|{[i,j]})|},

and hence the reciprocal of a positive integer.
∎

Remark 9.15
.

We considered here differentiability properties
for a last passage percolation problem.
Differentiability properties of a first passage percolation
model were studied in
[
93
]
.

10
Perfect simulation aspects of charged graphs

We now turn our attention to charged graphs with edge charge distribution
F
:
to every pair of
(i,j)
of integers, with
i<j
, assign a charge

w_{i,j}
with distribution
F
, independently.
For a path
\pi=(i_{0},\ldots,i_{\ell})
, that is, a finite increasing sequence
of integers, assign charge
w(\pi)=w_{i_{0},i_{1}}+\cdots+w_{i_{\ell-1},i_{\ell}}
,
and let

W_{i,j}=\sup\{w(\pi):\text{ $\pi$ is a path from $i$ to $j$}\}.

(10.1)

We use the notation
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},F)
for this random charged graph.
Thus,
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p\delta_{1}+(1-p)\delta_{x})
is another notation
the graph dealt with in
Section
9
, while
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p\delta_{1}+(1-p)\delta_{-\infty})

is another notation for the Barak-Erdős graph
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
.
Superadditivity inequality implies that there is a deterministic

C(F)
such that

\frac{W_{0,n}}{n}\to C(F),\quad\text{ a.s. as }n\to\infty.

In Section
9
we studied
C(p,x)\equiv C(p\delta_{1}+(1-p)\delta_{x})
as a function of
x
.
To deal with
C(F)
as a function of
F
analytically
is beyond the scope of this survey. We will, however, explain how
to approach this problem from an algorithmic view point, one
that allows to draw conjectures by simulating the graph
as accurately as possible.

As explained at the start of Section
9
, we will assume that

\operatorname{esssup}w_{i,j}>0
,
else we are dealing with a first passage percolation problem.
We will also assume, for convenience, that this essential supremum is finite
so, without loss of generality, let

\operatorname{esssup}w_{i,j}=\inf\{z:F((z,\infty))=0\}=1.

(10.2)

See Remark
10.8
below that explains that the finiteness of
\operatorname{esssup}w_{i,j}
is not a problem insofar as the perfect simulation is concerned.
Comparing
F
with the distribution
p\delta_{1/2}+(1-p)\delta_{-\infty}

with
p=F([1/2,1])
we obtain that

C(F)>0.

The goal of this section is the construction of a random variable
whose expectation is
C(F)
and such that this random variable
can be
perfectly simulated
.
A survey of perfect simulation
can be found in
[
64
]
. Its
relation to the so-called backwards-coupling was studied in
[
48
]
. It
belongs to the broader area of coupling methods for stochastic recursions that
may entirely lack the Markovian property
[
22
,
31
,
44
]
.

To do this we discuss an auxiliary Markovian
particle system, called Max Growth System (MGS),
that is an analog of the Infinite Bin Model.
Our reference throughout this section is
[
46
]
.

10.1
The Max Growth System (MGS)

The content of this subsection is purely deterministic.
Consider point measures on the set
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
that will
be referred to as “space”:

\mathcal{N}:=\{\text{locally finite point measures $\nu$ on }\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}\text{ such that }\nu(\mathbb{R}_{+})<\infty\}.

For example,
\nu=2\delta_{1}+\delta_{-1.5}+3\delta_{-4}+\delta_{-\infty}\in\mathcal{N}
.
We think of
\nu
as consisting of
7
particles, such that
two of them are at
1
, one at
