-1.5
, three at
-4
and one at
-\infty
;
so we can equivalently represent
\nu
by the locations of
its particles
(1,1,-1.5,-4,-4,-4,-\infty)
arranged in decreasing order.
In general, we let
\nu_{1}\geq\nu_{2}\geq\cdots

be the locations of the particles of
\nu
.
So the function

\mathcal{N}\ni\nu\mapsto\nu_{k}:=\text{ location of $k$th largest particle of $\nu$}

is well-defined for each
k
and
\nu=\sum_{k\geq 1}\delta_{\nu_{k}}
.
We also let

\|\nu\|:=\nu(\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}),\quad\inf\nu:=\nu_{\|\nu\|},

with
\inf\nu=-\infty
if
\|\nu\|=\infty
.
Next let

\mathcal{W}:=\big\{w=(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots):\,\sup_{k\geq 1}w_{k}\leq 1,\;w_{k}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}\text{ for all }k\big\},

and define

\mathfrak{m}(\nu,w):=\sup_{k\geq 1}(\nu_{k}+w_{k}),\quad\nu\neq 0,\quad w\in\mathcal{W}.

The map responsible for the dynamics of the MGS is
defined by

\Psi_{w}\nu:=\nu+\delta_{\mathfrak{m}(\nu,w)}.

Composing these maps, for possibly different
w
each time,
gives a trajectory in
\mathcal{N}
.
More precisely, the MGS starting at “time”
T
from state
\nu(T)\in\mathbb{N}

and driving sequence
w(t)
,
t>T
, is the sequence defined by

\nu(t)=\Psi_{w(t)}\nu(t-1),\quad t>T,

that is,

\nu(t)=\Psi_{w(t)}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\Psi_{w(t-1)}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\cdots\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\Psi_{w(T+1)}\nu(T).

Let
\mathcal{N}_{0}
consist of those
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
with
\nu_{1}=0
.
Define the map

\sigma:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{N}_{0},\quad\sigma\nu=\sum_{k\geq 1}\delta_{\nu_{k}-\nu_{1}}

that places the origin of space at
\nu_{1}
.
We will need the following easily verified properties.

\displaystyle\sigma\Psi_{w}=\sigma\Psi_{w}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu,w)=\mathfrak{m}(\nu,w)-\nu_{1}.

We next consider an MGS
\nu(t)
,
t\geq 0
, and show that under certain conditions
on the driving sequence the quantity

\mathfrak{M}(t)\equiv\mathfrak{m}(\nu(t-1),w(t))

will eventually not
depend on the choice of the initial state
\nu(0)
.

Lemma 10.1

(Decoupling property)
.

Fix
\ell\in[0,1)
.
Consider two MGSs,
\nu
,
\tilde{\nu}
, starting at time
0

with
\nu(0)\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
and
\tilde{\nu}(0)=\delta_{0}
.
Assume that the driving sequence
w(t)
,
t>0
, is the same for both.
Let
\mathfrak{M}(t)=\mathfrak{m}(\nu(t-1),w(t))
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}(t)=\mathfrak{m}(\tilde{\nu}(t-1),w(t))
.
If

\displaystyle\nu_{2}(0)

\displaystyle\leq-\ell,

(10.3)

\displaystyle\bar{w}(t)

\displaystyle:=\max\{w_{1}(t),\ldots,w_{t}(t)\}\geq 1-\ell\quad\text{ for all $1\leq t\leq n$,}

(10.4)

then

\mathfrak{M}(t)=\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}(t)\quad\text{ for all $1\leq t\leq n$.}

(10.5)

Proof.

We have
\nu_{1}(0)=0\geq-\ell\geq\nu_{2}(0)
, by assumption.
We will show by induction that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\text{if $\bar{w}(1),\ldots,\bar{w}(n)\geq 1-\ell$ }\text{ then }\begin{cases}\mathfrak{M}(n)=\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}(n)\\
\nu(n)|_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\tilde{\nu}(n)|_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\\
\nu(n)(\mathbb{R}_{+})=n+1\\
\nu_{n+2}(n)\leq-\ell\end{cases}

(10.6)

Let
n=1
and assume that
\bar{w}(1)=w_{1}(1)\geq 1-\ell
.
We have

\mathfrak{M}(1)=\max\{\nu_{1}(0)+w_{1}(1),\nu_{2}(0)+w_{2}(1),\ldots\}=w_{1}(1)
because
\nu_{1}(0)+w_{1}(1)=w_{1}(1)\geq 1-\ell

and since
1\geq w_{j}(1)
,
-\ell\geq\nu_{j}(0)
for all
j\geq 2
,
we have
w_{1}(1)\geq w_{j}(1)+\nu_{j}(0)
for all
j\geq 2
and so
only the first term survives in the maximum.
But then
\mathfrak{M}(1)=w_{1}(1)=\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}(1)
.
Since
\nu(1)=\nu(0)+\delta_{\mathfrak{M}(1)}
we have that its
restriction on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
equals
\delta_{0}+\delta_{w_{1}(1)}

and
\nu_{3}(1)=\nu_{2}(0)\leq-\ell
. So (
10.6
) holds when
n=1
.

Assume next (
10.6
) holds for some
n
. We show its veracity for
n+1
.
We work under the assumption that
\bar{w}(1),\ldots,\bar{w}(n),\bar{w}(n+1)\geq 1-\ell
.
By (
10.6
) we have

\nu(n)|_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\tilde{\nu}(n)|_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
(containing
n+1
particles
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
)
and

\nu_{n+1}(n-1)\leq-\ell
.
So

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}(n+1)

\displaystyle=\max_{j\geq 1}\{\nu_{j}(n)+w_{j}(n+1)\}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\leq n+1}\{\nu_{j}(n)+w_{j}(n+1)\}\vee\max_{j>n+1}\{\nu_{j}(n)+w_{j}(n+1)\}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\leq n+1}\{\tilde{\nu}_{j}(n)+w_{j}(n+1)\}\vee\max_{j>n+1}\{\nu_{j}(n)+w_{j}(n+1)\}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\leq n+1}\{\tilde{\nu}_{j}(n)+w_{j}(n+1)\}=\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}(n+1).

The reason that we dropped the second maximum is that

\max_{j\leq n+1}\{\tilde{\nu}_{j}(n-1)+w_{j}(n)\}\geq\max_{j\leq n+1}w_{j}(n)\geq 1-\ell

while
\max_{j>n+1}\{\nu_{j}(n-1)+w_{j}(n)\}\leq\nu_{n+1}(n-1)+\max_{j>n+1}w_{j}(n)\leq-\ell+1
.
We also have
\nu(n+1)=\nu(n)+\delta_{\mathfrak{M}(n+1)}=\nu(n)+\delta_{\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}(n+1)}

so
\nu(n+1)|_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\tilde{\nu}(n+1)|_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}

and they have
n+2
particles on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
while
\nu_{n+3}(n+1)=\nu_{n+2}(n)\leq-\ell
.
∎

Remark 10.2
.

Lemma
10.1
and the conclusion (
10.5
)
can easily be modified if we need to start the MGS
from an arbitrary time
T
rather than
T=0
.
The quantity that replaces
\overline{w}(t)
will be

\bar{w}(T;t):=\max\{w_{1}(T+t),\ldots,w_{t}(T+t)\}.

(10.7)

This is needed in Theorem
10.6
below.

Remark 10.3
.

The decoupling property is essentially responsible for
producing, in a stochastic version of the MGS, the so-called
renovating events that constitute a nice way for exhibiting
stability. The original theory can be found in

[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
and its extension for
functional of stochastic dynamical systems in
[
45
]
.

10.2
The MGS of a random charged graph, coupling and stationarity

Definition 10.4
.

Given a probability measure
Q
on
\mathcal{W}

define the random process MGS
(Q)
by

\nu(t)=\Psi_{w(t)}\nu(t-1)
,
t\geq 1
, with

w(1),w(2),\ldots
being independent with common law
Q
,
with
\nu(0)\in\mathcal{N}
arbitrary.
Clearly, MGS
(Q)
is Markovian.
In the particular case where the components of
w(1)
are i.i.d. with common distribution
F
we shall be using the notation
MGS
(Q_{F})
.

Let
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},F)
be a random charged graph with charge distribution
F

satisfying (
10.2
).
Let

W_{n}:=W_{0,n},

in the notation of (
10.1
).
In particular,
W_{0}=0
,
W_{1}=w_{0,1}
,
W_{2}=(w_{0,1}+w_{1,2})\vee w_{1,2}
.
Let

\nu^{G}(n):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\delta_{W_{k}},\quad n\geq 0.

(10.8)

Then
\nu^{G}(n)\in\mathcal{N}
and has
n+1
particles.
Clearly,
\nu^{G}(n)=\nu^{G}(n-1)+\delta_{W_{n}}
,
with
W_{n}=\max_{0\leq k\leq n-1}(W_{k}+w_{k,n})=\max_{k}(\nu^{G}_{k}(n-1)+w_{\pi(k),n})
where
\pi
is a
permutation on
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
that puts

W_{0},\ldots,W_{n-1}
in decreasing order.
Hence
W_{n}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\max_{k}(\nu^{G}_{k}(n-1)+w_{k,n})

and so if we consider the MGS
(Q_{F})
defined by

\nu(n)=\nu(n-1)+\delta_{\mathfrak{m}(\nu(n-1),w(n))},

starting with
\nu(0)=\delta_{0}
,
we see that

\nu^{G}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\nu,

(10.9)

not only component-wise, but also as processes.
Therefore, letting

M_{n}=\max_{0\leq k\leq n}W_{k}

(10.10)

we have

(M_{n}-M_{n-1},n\geq 1)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(n-1),w(n))^{+},n\geq 1),

which implies that
M_{n}/n
is a sample mean:

\frac{M_{n}}{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(M_{j}-M_{j-1})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(j-1),w(j))^{+},

where the last equality in distribution is at the level of sequences.
Since

C(F)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{W_{n}}{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{M_{n}}{n}\text{ a.s.},

and since
\int_{0}^{\infty}xF(dx)<\infty
, we have

C(F)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}M_{n}}{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(j-1),w(j))^{+}.

Remark 10.5
.

Let us make the reasonable ansatz that the quantity inside the last
expectation converges in distribution to some random variable:

\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(n-1),w(n))^{+}\xrightarrow{\text{\rm d}}\bar{\mathfrak{m}}^{+}\text{ as }n\to\infty.

(10.11)

Then, a poor man’s estimate of
C(F)
would be to simply to
compute sample means

n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(j-1),w(j))^{+}

for large
n
.
But the sample means are not from i.i.d. copies of the limiting
variable and thus the sampling is “not perfect”.
To achieve perfect sampling, we must first show that the non-Markovian process

\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(n-1),w(n))
,
n=1,2,\ldots
, has a stationary
version
(we say that a process has a stationary version if it is eventually
equal to a stationary process; the “eventually” means that there
is a finite random time after which the two processes are equal a.s.)
from which we can sample i.i.d. copies constructively.

Theorem 10.6
.

Suppose that
F
satisfies (
10.2
).
Let
\nu
be an MGS
(Q_{F})
with driving sequence
w(t)
,
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
starting from an arbitrary state at an arbitrary, possibly random, time.
Then there exists a stationary process
(\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(t),t\in\mathbb{Z})
such that

\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(t-1),w(t))=\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(t)\text{ for $t$ large enough, a.s.}

In particular
\mathbb{E}(\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)^{+})=C(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in[0,1)
be such that
p:=F([1-\ell,1])>0
,
which is possible due to (
10.2
).
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, consider the event

R_{k}:=\{w_{1}(k)\geq\ell\}\cap\bigcap\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}\big\{\max(w_{1}(k+j),\ldots,w_{j}(k+j))\geq 1-\ell\big\}.

It is clear from its definition that
(R_{k},k\in\mathbb{Z})
is
a stationary sequence of events with

\mathbb{P}(R_{k})=\mathbb{P}(R_{0})=F([\ell,1])\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}(1-(1-p)^{j})>0.

Consider the stationary random set
J:=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:R_{k}\text{ holds}\}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}(R_{k})>0
, we have, by ergodicity (more specifically by the Poincaré
recurrence theorem),

\inf J=-\infty
and
\sup J=\infty
a.s. We enumerate the elements of
J
by

\cdots<T_{-1}<T_{0}\leq 0<T_{1}<T_{2}<\cdots

Hence the
R_{T_{i}}
are all events of probability
1
.
We define
\tilde{\nu}(t)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, by letting, for all
j
,

\tilde{\nu}(T_{j})=\delta_{0},\qquad\tilde{\nu}(t)=\sigma\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{T_{i}<t<T_{i+1}\}}\Psi^{t}_{w(t)}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\cdots\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\Psi^{T_{i}+1}_{w(T_{i}+1)}\delta_{0},\quad t\not=T_{j}.

On the other hand, thanks to Lemma
10.1
, the
process
\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(t-1),w(t))
,
t>T_{i}
, is algebraically
independent of
\nu(T_{i})
.
This shows, see
[
44
]
, that the stationary process

\bar{m}
defined by

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(t)=\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\tilde{\nu}(t-1),w(t)),\quad t\in\mathbb{Z},

satisfies

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(t)=\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(t-1),w(t))
for all
t
large enough, a.s.
Next, using that

C(F)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}(M_{n})}{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(j-1),w(j))^{+})\quad\text{a.s.},

and using the eventual equality between
\mathfrak{m}(\sigma\nu(t-1),w(t))
and

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(t)
, we have

C(F)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(j)^{+})=\mathbb{E}(\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)^{+}),

by stationarity and ergodicity of the sequence.
∎

10.3
Perfect simulation

Recall the quantity
\overline{w}
defined by (
10.7
).

Theorem 10.7

(Perfect simulation)
.

Define

T^{*}:=\sup\{t\leq-1:\,{w}_{1}(t)\geq\ell,\min_{1\leq j\leq|t|}\overline{w}(t;j)\geq 1-\ell\}.

Then
|T^{*}|<\infty
a.s., and

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)=\mathfrak{m}\left(\sigma\Psi^{T^{*}+1}_{w(T^{*}+1})\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\cdots\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\Psi^{0}_{w(0)}\delta_{0};w(0)\right)\text{ a.s.}

Proof.

We recall that
(T_{-j},j\in\mathbb{N})
are the negative elements of the random set
J
, with
T_{-1}>-\infty
. We remark that

w_{1}(T_{-1})\geq\ell,\quad\bar{w}(T_{-1};j)\geq 1-\ell\text{ for all $j>0$},

therefore
T_{*}\geq T_{-1}
, proving its finiteness.

Moreover, since

w_{1}(T^{*})\geq\ell,\min_{1\leq j\leq|t|}\bar{w}(T^{*};j)\geq 1-\ell,

by Lemma
10.1
, the quantity

\mathfrak{m}\left(\sigma\Psi^{T^{*}+1}_{w(T^{*}+1})\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\cdots\raisebox{0.43057pt}{\scriptsize$\circ$}\Psi^{0}_{w(0)}\nu;w(0)\right)

does not algebraically depend on the value of

\nu\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
. As a result, it is equal to
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)
.
∎

Fix
t=0
and
J=1
;

Generate the variable
w_{1}(0)
;

Fix Stopping = False;

while

Stopping = False

do

while

\max_{1\leq j\leq J}w_{j}(t)<1-\ell

do

Increase
J
by
1
;

Generate the variable
w_{J}(t)
;

while

J>1

do

Decrease
J
by
1
and
t
by
1
;

Generate
w_{1}(t),\ldots w_{J}(t)
;

while

\max_{1\leq j\leq J}w_{j}(t)<1-\ell

do

Increase
J
by
1
;

Generate the variable
w_{J}(t)
;

Decrease
t
by
1
;

Generate
w_{1}(t)
;

Fix Stopping =
\{w_{1}(t)\geq\ell\}
;

Fix
\nu=\delta_{0}
;

for

s
from
t+1
to
-1

do

Generate the variables
w_{1}(s),\ldots,w_{||\nu||}(s)
;

Set
\mathfrak{m}=\max\{\nu_{j}+w_{j}(s)\text{ for $1\leq j\leq\|\nu\|$}\}
;

Add
\delta_{\mathfrak{m}}
to
\nu
;

Set
\mathfrak{m}=\max\{\nu_{j}+w_{j}(0)\text{ for }1\leq j\leq\|\nu\|\}
;

Return:

\mathfrak{m}-\nu_{1}
;

Algorithm 1

Construction of a variable of law
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)
.

We now describe more precisely the perfect simulation algorithm. Let
F
be a
probability distribution satisfying (
10.2
), we fix
\ell\in[0,1)
such that
F([1-\ell,1])\in(0,1)
. The algorithm requires the
construction of an array of i.i.d. random variables with common distribution
F

until the random variable
T^{*}
can be constructed.

To construct
T^{*}
as well as
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)
from the sequence
\{w_{j}(t),j\in\mathbb{N},t\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, one only needs to consider a.s. finitely many elements of this
set, as
\{T^{*}=t\}
is a measurable function of

\{w_{1}(t)\}\cup\{w_{j}(t+k),1\leq j\leq k\leq|t|\}

and
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)
is a measurable function of

\{w_{1}(T^{*})\}\cup\{w_{j}(T^{*}+k),1\leq j\leq k\leq|T^{*}|\}.

Therefore, we can explore triangular arrays of the form

\{w_{1}(t)\}\cup\{w_{j}(t+k),1\leq j\leq k\leq|t|\},

progressively decreasing
t
until time
T^{*}
is detected. Once this random
variable is known, we construct the random variable
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)
using the
procedure described in Theorem
10.6
from the previously discovered random
variables. A possible implementation is described in Algorithm
1
.
We show a graphical representation of a run of Algorithm
1
in
Figure
10
.

Figure 10
:
Illustration of the execution of Algorithm
1
on an
example, in the case where
\ell<1-\ell
. The variables sampled until the
Boolean variable Stopping becomes True are pictured by black/white squares and
disks. One searches for the first time
T^{*}
such that every line of index

T^{*}+1\leq t\leq 0
has at least one black disk between columns
1
and
t-T^{*}

and such that there is a black square in position
(T^{*},1)
. The full triangular
array of variables used in the construction of
\nu
is enclosed by a red
boundary.

We observe that this algorithm has a complexity of
(T^{*})^{2}
, as it is the
number of steps needed to generate the variable
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)
. It is worth
noting that
-T^{*}
can be constructed as the first hitting time of
0
of the
Markov chain
(X_{n})
with initial state

X_{0}=\min\{j\geq 1,w_{j}(0)\geq 1-\ell\}

and with transition probabilities defined for all
j\geq 2
and
i\geq j
by

P(j,j-1)=1-(1-p)^{j-1}\text{ and }P(j,i)=p(1-p)^{i-1}

where
p=\mathbb{P}(w_{1}(0)\geq 1-\ell)
, with

P(1,0)=\mathbb{P}(w_{1}(0)\geq\ell),\quad\mathbb{P}(1,1)=\mathbb{P}(1-\ell\leq w_{1}(0)<\ell),\quad P(1,j)=p(1-p)^{j-1}\text{ for }j\geq 2.

The quantity
X_{n}
corresponds to the value of the variable
J
at the end of
the period when
t=-n
in Algorithm
1
. In the example shown in
Figure
10
, we have

(X_{0},X_{-1},X_{-2},X_{-3},X_{-4},X_{-5},X_{-6})=(4,3,2,1,2,1,0).

Note that
T^{*}
has exponential tails.

The choice of the parameter
\ell
may have an important effect on the behavior
of the average complexity
\mathbb{E}((T^{*})^{2})
of the algorithm. We plotted
\ell\mapsto\mathbb{E}((T^{*})^{2})
in Figure
11
, when the charge
distribution is given by
F(\mathrm{d}x)={\text{\Large$\mathfrak{1}$}_{\left\{x\leq 1\right\}}}e^{x-1}\mathrm{d}x
.
Additionally, as
p\to 0
, the quantity
\mathbb{E}((T^{*})^{2})
grows to
\infty
. We
estimated
\mathbb{E}((T^{*})^{2})
for
F=p\delta_{1}+(1-p)\delta_{-\infty}
and plotted
this quantity as a function of
p
in Figure
12
.

Figure 11
:
Dependency in the parameter
\ell
of the complexity of Algorithm

1
with a charge distribution
F(\mathrm{d}x)={\text{\Large$\mathfrak{1}$}_{\left\{x\leq 1\right\}}}e^{x-1}\mathrm{d}x
. The figure was obtained with a Monte Carlo simulation of

N=10^{4}
copies of
T^{*}
for
100
different values of
\ell
. For this charge
distribution, the
Monte Carlo simulations give
C(F)=0.4432\pm 0.0006
.

We observe in Figure
11
that different choices of the
value
\ell
can have a dramatic impact on the efficiency of Algorithm

1
. Choosing a value
\ell
too small has the effect of making the
first appearance of a triangular event too late. On the other hand, if
\ell
is
too big then with high probability, one will have
w_{1}(T)\leq\ell
, and thus
the first “successful” triangular event will appear much later. For the
distribution
F
we chose, it appears that an optimal choice of
\ell
seems to
be around
\ell=0.7
, which balances between these two extremes.

Figure 12
:
Dependency in the parameter
p
of the complexity of Algorithm

1
applied to the detection of the longest path in the
Barak-Erdős graph with parameter
p
. Figure obtained through Monte Carlo
simulation of
N=10^{5}
copies of
T^{*}
for 120 different values of
p
.

We observe in Figure
12
that if
F
puts a large mass on
the negative half-line, the complexity of Algorithm
1
can become
quite large. The function
p\mapsto\mathbb{E}(T^{*})^{2}
grows at least exponentially in

1/p
as
p\to 0
in the Barak-Erdős graph, but we were not able to obtain
a good estimate of this rate of increase.

Remark 10.8

(Perfect simulation when the charge distribution has an infinite
essential supremum)
.

We explained how to perfectly simulate the random variable

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)^{+}
whose expectation is
C(F)
under the assumption
that the supremum of the support of
F
is
1
or any finite number for that
matter.
To perfectly simulate a random variable whose expectation is
C(F)
when
the supremum of the support of
F
is
\infty
, we use an idea by Glynn and
Rhee
[
54
]
.
Denote by
X_{n}
the variable
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(0)^{+}
when
F=F_{n}
has
n
as
the supremum of its support
and note that
X_{n}\to X
a.s. and in
L^{1}
for some random variable
X
.
Then
C(F_{n})=\mathbb{E}X_{n}\to\mathbb{E}X=C(F)
, as
n\to\infty
.
We may not be able to perfectly simulate
X
. However,
if we let
\nu
be a positive random integer such that
p_{n}=\mathbb{P}(\nu=n)>0

for all
n
and set
Y=(X_{\nu}-X_{\nu-1})/p_{\nu}
then
Y
can be perfectly
simulated. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}Y=C(F)
.
This is because
\mathbb{E}Y=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}p_{n}\mathbb{E}(Y|\nu=n)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(X_{n}-X_{n-1})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}X_{n}=\mathbb{E}X
.

Remark 10.9
.

For the special case of a Barak-Erdős graph, a simpler
perfect simulation algorithm is explained in
[
44
]
.

11
Additional remarks and open problems

The last passage percolation constant
C(p)
for
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
has been
one of the main concerns in this survey.
We showed how very good approximations can be obtained analytically
by relating
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
to the IBM
(p)
model.
The function
C(p)
is not convex. We conjecture however that
C(p)/p
is.

The passage to the IBM enables using branching processes techniques
that help explain the slow rate of convergence of
C(p)/p
to
e

as
p\to 0
and its relation to the Brunet-Derrida behavior.
Such phenomena should be present when the geometric
(p)
,
0<p<1
,
distributions in the IBM
are replaced by a family of more general distributions.

The CLTs are generally available for the length of the longest or
heaviest path but, as explained, some moment conditions are needed
in case random weights are added to the edges.
There is one thing that is not totally clear in the CLTs and this is
the variance parameter. In, e.g., the case of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z},p)
, we have
that
(L_{n}-Cn)/\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\lambda n}
converges in
distribution to a standard normal with
\sigma^{2}
being equal to
the variance of
L_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}-C(\Gamma_{2}-\Gamma_{2})
. This
quantity is unknown and not even bounds are available. The situation becomes
more complex in presence of weights.

CLTs are also available for the IBM and can take various forms.
For example, for the IBM
(p)
model, it has been shown that

\sqrt{n}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{[A_{n}t]}X_{n}(k)-t\right)
,
0\leq t\leq 1
,
converges in law to a Brownian motion if
A_{n}=nC
and to a Brownian bridge
if
A_{n}=L_{n}
. See
[
44
, Thm. 9.2]
.

When considering random directed graphs on partially ordered vertex sets,
the CLT limits are not necessarily Gaussian. The Brownian last passage
percolation process
Z(t)=\max_{0\leq s\leq t}(X(s)+Y(t)-Y(s))

pops up for example in the case of the vertex set
\mathbb{Z}\times\{1,2\}
.
We know nothing about the random variable
S=S(t)
that achieves this maximum.
This is of interest as it would be related to the path that achieves the
maximum.

Concerning longest paths of
\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z},p)
on a
window
[0,n]\times[0,m]
, as
n,m=m(n)\to\infty
,
the limit theorem of (
7.6
) is not optimal. We conjecture that
the Tracy-Widom limit is also possible when
m(n)
grows with
n
linearly.
One can of course ask for analogous results in the

\overrightarrow{G}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},p)
case, when
d\geq 3
. It is not clear at all what
kind of laws would replace the Tracy-Widom distribution here.

The recent paper of Terlat
[
94
]
makes progress in the behavior
of
C(F)
when
F
is a distribution whose support may include negative
numbers with a possible atom on
-\infty
. Concerning however the
type of limit theorems that one can obtain when
F
is heavy-tailed,
such as those of Section (
8.2
), the field is open.

Concerning other types of behavior of maximal lengths or weights,
e.g., in the large deviations sense, we mention that,
when weights have a distribution supported on the integers,
normal and moderate large deviations were obtained in
[
68
]
.
In addition local limit theorems were obtained when the weight distribution
is non-lattice.

Distributional results for maximal/heaviest weighted paths are not available
beyond results around CLTs. However, in the sparse case, one can
use the recursive nature of the (possibly weighted) PWIT to come
up with functional equations for such quantities. See
e.g.
[
45
, eq. (13)]
.

Recall that
C(p,x)
of Section
9
,
is the last passage percolation constant when the weight law is

p\delta_{1}+(1-p)\delta_{x}
for all edges
(i,j)
with
i<j
.
There is only one solvable model we are aware of, that of
C(p,0)

as in
[
41
]
. Can there be other ones?

If we replace
p\delta_{1}+(1-p)\delta_{x}
above
by
F_{p,x}=pQ+(1-p)\delta_{x}
where
Q
is a probability measure on
(0,\infty)

we expect that the behavior of
p\mapsto C(F_{p,x})
can be obtained
by techniques similar to those of
[
79
,
80
]
, so long as

\int y^{2}Q(dy)<\infty
.
As a function of
x
, the
C(F_{p,x})
quantity is continuous and
convex and it can be seen that
C(F_{p,x})
is not differentiable at
x

iff
Q
has an atom
y
such that
x/y\in\mathbb{X}_{\text{\rm crit}}
.
See
[
47
, Sec. 5]
.
